#10597. How to watch your Xbox Playback recap for the 25th anniversary

Xbox flung open its archives ahead of its 25th birthday with a recap built to dredge up every hour you’ve sunk in.

That recap filled a gap players noticed last year, when Xbox quietly skipped its annual Year in Review and left fans leaning on third-party trackers. A late-October leak hinted at a comeback, and it finally arrived in a packed anniversary week, a day before the $899.99 Series X25 lands on November 13.

Only this time, Xbox went bigger with the launch of Xbox Playback, a personalized recap that covers every console generation instead of a single year, on November 12. It goes back as far as the Duke controller era and serves up a smorgasbord of stats, including your first Xbox game, total time played, your most-played game, your top years and your longest streak.

You can watch your Playback in a few ways:

To qualify, your profile needs at least one Gamerscore, one achievement and one minute of qualifying gameplay. Xbox is rolling the feature out gradually throughout the day, so it might not show up right away. Plus, because it relies on available account data, parts of older histories may also be incomplete or take time to appear.

In any case, there’s time. Playback runs until December 31 in 15 countries, including the US, UK, Spain, Brazil, and Australia.

Xbox started recap season early, while Sony has usually followed in December. If you play on both consoles, our guide to the 2025 PlayStation Wrap-Up explains how last year’s version worked.

#10594. Genshin Impact cheater offering paid carry services reportedly arrested while meeting with customers

A Genshin Impact player in China has reportedly been arrested after allegedly using cheats to help customers complete difficult in-game challenges in exchange for money.

The player is accused of running a paid boosting service for Stygian Onslaught, one of Genshin Impact’s toughest combat challenges, using unauthorized tools to clear content for other players.

However, the alleged operation came to an abrupt end when police reportedly detained the individual during a meeting with customers inside a clothing store.

According to Enduins, the player had been advertising their services through videos on the Chinese platform Bilibili, demonstrating how cheats could be used to complete Stygian Onslaught.

The challenge requires players to complete three timed battles, with higher difficulties offering significantly tougher encounters.

The allegations began circulating on Chinese gaming forum NGA before being shared by Seele Leaks on Telegram, alongside images documenting the incident.

One image appears to show several individuals gathered inside a clothing store in security camera footage dated November 3, 2026. Another shows a Chinese-language apology statement displayed on a phone.

The player subsequently apologized to HoYoverse and removed posts advertising their services.

“I used this account without authorization to publicly post videos of myself using cheats in Genshin Impact. I then directed viewers from Bilibili to my Xianyu secondhand marketplace store where I offered paid game boosting services,” they wrote.

“My actions seriously violated miHoYo’s and Genshin Impact’s official rules prohibiting cheating. They infringed upon miHoYo’s intellectual property rights and disrupted the game’s operations, fairness, and the gaming experience of other players.”

Genshin Impact devs vow legal action against cheaters ruining game

Genshin Impact players review bomb HoYoverse office on Google Maps over reward backlash

Genshin Impact “secretly” sent currency to streamers to get characters

This wouldn’t be the first time HoYoverse has taken serious action against cheating. In March 2026, the developer revealed that it had removed more than one million illegal links and pieces of content during 2025, including cheat software and pirated game resources.

The company also previously threatened legal action against Genshin Impact cheaters in 2023 after malicious plug-ins were used to interfere with other players’ accounts.

China has also seen legal consequences for paid boosting operations. In September 2025, a Shanghai court ordered a Genshin Impact boosting studio to pay 3 million yuan in damages after finding that it had used cheats to provide services to customers.

For the latest alleged offender, what started as a way to make money clearing Genshin Impact challenges may now have become a much more serious legal matter.

#10592. Brain inspired machines are better at math than expected

Computers designed to mimic the structure of the human brain are showing an unexpected strength. They can solve some of the demanding mathematical equations that lie at the heart of major scientific and engineering problems.

In a study published in Nature Machine Intelligence, Sandia National Laboratories computational neuroscientists Brad Theilman and Brad Aimone introduced a new algorithm that allows neuromorphic hardware to solve partial differential equations, or PDEs -- the mathematical foundation for modeling phenomena such as fluid dynamics, electromagnetic fields and structural mechanics.

The results demonstrate that neuromorphic systems can handle these equations efficiently. The advance could help open the door to the first neuromorphic supercomputer, offering a new path toward energy efficient computing for national security and other critical applications.

The research was funded by the Department of Energy's Office of Science through the Advanced Scientific Computing Research and Basic Energy Sciences programs, as well as the National Nuclear Security Administration's Advanced Simulation and Computing program.

Solving Partial Differential Equations With Brain Like Hardware

Partial differential equations are essential for simulating real world systems. They are used to forecast weather, analyze how materials respond to stress, and model complex physical processes. Traditionally, solving PDEs requires enormous computing power. Neuromorphic computers approach the problem differently by processing information in ways that resemble how the brain operates.

"We're just starting to have computational systems that can exhibit intelligent-like behavior. But they look nothing like the brain, and the amount of resources that they require is ridiculous, frankly," Theilman said.

For years, neuromorphic systems were mainly viewed as tools for pattern recognition or for speeding up artificial neural networks. Few expected them to manage mathematically rigorous problems such as PDEs, which are typically handled by large scale supercomputers.

Aimone and Theilman were not surprised by the outcome. They argue that the human brain routinely carries out highly complex calculations, even if people are unaware of it.

"Pick any sort of motor control task -- like hitting a tennis ball or swinging a bat at a baseball," Aimone said. "These are very sophisticated computations. They are exascale-level problems that our brains are capable of doing very cheaply."

Energy Efficient Computing for National Security

The findings could have major implications for the National Nuclear Security Administration, which is responsible for maintaining the nation's nuclear deterrent. Supercomputers used across the nuclear weapons complex consume vast amounts of electricity to simulate the physics of nuclear systems and other high stakes scenarios.

Neuromorphic computing may provide a way to significantly cut energy use while still delivering strong computational performance. By solving PDEs in a brain inspired manner, these systems suggest that large simulations could be run using far less power than conventional supercomputers require.

"You can solve real physics problems with brain-like computation," Aimone said. "That's something you wouldn't expect because people's intuition goes the opposite way. And in fact, that intuition is often wrong."

The team envisions neuromorphic supercomputers eventually becoming central to Sandia's mission of protecting national security.

What Neuromorphic Computing Reveals About the Brain

Beyond engineering advances, the research also touches on deeper questions about intelligence and how the brain performs calculations. The algorithm developed by Theilman and Aimone closely mirrors the structure and behavior of cortical networks.

"We based our circuit on a relatively well-known model in the computational neuroscience world," Theilman said. "We've shown the model has a natural but non-obvious link to PDEs, and that link hasn't been made until now -- 12 years after the model was introduced."

The researchers believe this work could help connect neuroscience with applied mathematics, offering new understanding of how the brain processes information.

"Diseases of the brain could be diseases of computation," Aimone said. "But we don't have a solid grasp on how the brain performs computations yet."

If that idea proves correct, neuromorphic computing might one day contribute to better understanding and treatment of neurological disorders such as Alzheimer's and Parkinson's.

Building the Next Generation of Supercomputers

Neuromorphic computing remains an emerging field, but this work represents an important step forward. The Sandia team hopes their results will encourage collaboration among mathematicians, neuroscientists and engineers to expand what this technology can achieve.

"If we've already shown that we can import this relatively basic but fundamental applied math algorithm into neuromorphic -- is there a corresponding neuromorphic formulation for even more advanced applied math techniques?" Theilman said.

As development continues, the researchers are optimistic. "We have a foot in the door for understanding the scientific questions, but also we have something that solves a real problem," Theilman said.

Materials provided by DOE/Sandia National Laboratories. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10590. Building a Human Review Pipeline for AI Search: A Practical Developer’s Guide

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

An AI search feature can return valid JSON, render correctly, and still give an unhelpful answer.

Imagine a documentation assistant answering a question about an API's current authentication requirements. It retrieves an outdated page, summarizes it fluently, and displays a citation. Every technical component appears to work, but the user receives instructions that no longer apply.

Automated checks are valuable, yet they cannot replace a clearly defined judgment about whether an answer solves the user's problem.

This guide builds a human-review workflow for a hypothetical API documentation assistant. You will define a review rubric, validate submissions with Python, measure reviewer disagreement, and turn accepted judgments into regression cases. The Python examples use Pydantic 2 and scikit-learn; they do not require a model-provider API key.

Start by identifying the failure you want to detect.

A documentation assistant usually has several stages: receive a question, retrieve relevant documents, generate an answer, and present that answer with supporting material.

Do not collapse these into a single “good answer” rating. A low score without a reason tells the team little about what to fix.

SitePoint’s introduction to retrieval-augmented generation provides background on how retrieval and generation fit together.

Preserve the exact question, answer revision, retrieved passages, document versions, and retrieval timestamp for each run. Store a snapshot manifest with stable passage IDs, source URLs, and content hashes. Reviewers should see the material supplied to that run, not today’s edited page. Keep the model and prompt configuration with the run so a later review can be traced to the behavior it evaluated.

A useful rubric defines observable criteria and separates evidence support from task completion and source suitability. Give each review the same user task and expected API version. Otherwise, reviewers may judge different questions while appearing to disagree about one answer.

For an answer about API authentication, use separate labels such as:

Supported: every material factual claim is backed by the supplied evidence.

Partially supported: some material claims lack support, while others are grounded.

Unsupported: the answer's central instruction lacks support or contradicts the evidence.

Cannot assess: the necessary evidence is missing, unreadable, or inaccessible.

Define task completion independently as complete, incomplete, or cannot assess. For source suitability, use suitable, outdated, or cannot assess. An answer can faithfully summarize an obsolete page while failing the request for current instructions.

Consider a fictional API migration: version 2 accepts X-API-Key, while version 3 requires Authorization: Bearer . If the supplied evidence contains only the version 2 page, an answer repeating X-API-Key can be supported by its evidence but still use an outdated source. If the evidence explicitly requires Bearer and the answer invents X-API-Key, label the answer unsupported. These are different engineering failures.

Also establish what reviewers should do when the source is outdated. They may correctly conclude that an answer reflects its evidence while separately flagging that evidence as unsuitable for the user's current task.

Start with a calibration batch covering clear successes, unsupported claims, missing evidence, and version mismatches. Have two reviewers label the same items independently, then compare their reasons. Revise ambiguous definitions before increasing volume; the batch is for improving the rubric, not estimating production quality.

Keep a rubric version on every review. Labels produced under different definitions should not be mixed without checking comparability.

A spreadsheet can help during exploration, but an engineering pipeline needs a stable record format.

Pydantic can validate a review record before it enters the dataset. Install Pydantic 2 and scikit-learn in your project environment, then use this model:

from pydantic import BaseModel, ConfigDict, Field, ValidationError

model_config = ConfigDict(extra="forbid", strict=True)

answer_revision: str = Field(min_length=1)

completion: Literal["complete", "incomplete", "cannot_assess"]

source_fit: Literal["suitable", "outdated", "cannot_assess"]

rationale: str = Field(min_length=20, max_length=2000)

Strict mode limits type coercion, and extra="forbid" rejects unexpected fields. The model checks shape and allowed values; it cannot establish that a judgment is correct. A minimum rationale length does not guarantee a useful explanation. The snapshot ID references a manifest, which can also record that retrieval produced no usable passages.

On the server, resolve the item, answer revision, snapshot, and passage IDs against the stored run. Reject IDs that do not belong together. Obtain the reviewer identity from the authenticated session rather than trusting a submitted reviewer_id. Store a server-generated submission time and preserve review revisions instead of silently overwriting earlier judgments.

Run this after the model definition. It accepts one fictional review and rejects both an unknown label and an extra field:

"rationale": "The answer invents X-API-Key; p1 requires Bearer.",

raise AssertionError("Invalid review was accepted")

The expected error types are literal_error and extra_forbidden. Add separate integration tests for mismatched evidence IDs, unauthorized submissions, and retries. Use a database uniqueness rule or an idempotency key so a repeated request does not create a second independent review.

More reviews do not automatically produce better evidence. If a task is ambiguous, scaling it can simply reproduce the ambiguity.

The workflow needs clear eligibility requirements, examples, escalation routes, and acceptance criteria. Reviewers assessing API behavior may need domain knowledge that general writing fluency does not provide.

If you work with an external AI-data contributor service such as Acquirox, define the task contract before assigning work: required expertise, permitted source material, output fields, and escalation rules. Keep ownership of the rubric, evaluation set, and acceptance decisions within the product team. Contributor capacity alone does not establish expertise in the API being reviewed.

Pilot the actual task in the review interface. Track drafts, submitted reviews, items needing adjudication, and final decisions as distinct workflow states. Reviewer judgments should remain immutable once submitted; corrections should create a new revision with a reason. Keep adjudication separate from the original labels.

For data involving users or contributors, document permitted uses, access boundaries, and retention. Remove credentials and unnecessary personal information from review material. An internal support transcript should not become a broadly accessible annotation task by accident.

Distinguish annotation from usability research. A skilled reviewer can judge whether an answer is supported, but that does not prove a first-time user can understand the interface.

SitePoint’s guide to choosing usability tests and participants offers related guidance on matching participants and methods to the question being investigated.

The review interface shapes the data it collects.

Label Studio is an option for configurable labeling interfaces and data import/export. If you use it, map exported annotations into the Review schema through an adapter; do not assume its export structure matches your application contract. A custom interface may be appropriate when reviewers need API-version navigation or passage-level citations.

The original question and relevant user context.

The evidence snapshot supplied to the model.

Separate controls for support and task completion.

A rationale field and an explicit “cannot assess” option.

Display stable passage IDs beside the evidence and let reviewers attach those IDs to their rationale. Show the source version and retrieval time near each passage. Render retrieved content as untrusted text or sanitized markup, and never treat instructions embedded in a source document as reviewer instructions. Avoid loading executable third-party content inside the review panel.

Avoid showing a preferred model name or a previous reviewer's verdict before an independent judgment. For pairwise comparisons, randomize answer order and include appropriate tie or neither-acceptable outcomes.

Design for keyboard use, visible focus, readable text, and explicit selection states. Keep evidence and the answer accessible without requiring excessive horizontal scrolling.

Start all judgment controls unselected and validate them on submission. Use labeled radio groups, an accessible error summary, and visible saving/submission status. A failed save should preserve the draft. Disable duplicate submissions in the UI, but enforce duplicate handling on the server as well.

Practical check: observe a reviewer completing several borderline examples. Record where they pause, misinterpret a label, or cannot find the necessary source material.

When two reviewers disagree, investigate before reducing their decisions to a majority vote.

Different assumptions about the user's expertise.

A genuine error by one or both reviewers.

Preserve independent labels and store the adjudicated result separately. This allows the team to study ambiguity instead of erasing it.

For two reviewers applying categorical labels to the same items, scikit-learn provides Cohen's kappa:

from sklearn.metrics import cohen_kappa_score

("q1", "a1", "s1", "auth-v1"): "supported",

("q2", "a1", "s2", "auth-v1"): "unsupported",

("q3", "a1", "s3", "auth-v1"): "supported",

("q4", "a1", "s4", "auth-v1"): "partially_supported"

("q3", "a1", "s3", "auth-v1"): "partially_supported"

raise ValueError("Review sets must contain identical items")

raw_agreement = sum(x == y for x, y in zip(left, right)) / len(keys)

print(f"Raw agreement: {raw_agreement:.2f}")

print(f"Kappa: {cohen_kappa_score(left, right):.2f}")

This toy example prints raw agreement of 0.75 and kappa of about 0.64. Four items are not enough to assess a review program. In production, check for duplicate reviews before building dictionaries, use one rubric version per comparison, report unmatched items, and align all records by item, answer revision, and evidence snapshot.

Kappa measures agreement beyond chance under its assumptions, not correctness. Inspect raw agreement, label frequencies, and the disagreement table alongside it. A single-category batch can make kappa undefined; report that limitation rather than substituting a reassuring score. Treat cannot_assess explicitly and report its frequency instead of silently excluding difficult cases.

If both reviewers apply the same flawed rubric consistently, agreement can be high while the evaluation remains unhelpful.

Once difficult cases are adjudicated, turn them into regression cases with explicit acceptance criteria. For the fictional authentication task: use version 3 evidence, cite the passage requiring Bearer, and do not invent a header. If version 3 evidence is missing, an acceptable response should explain the gap rather than provide confident instructions.

Expected behavior or acceptance criteria.

DVC can track larger datasets alongside Git. In an existing Git repository with DVC initialized and data/reviewed-auth.jsonl present, a minimal versioning step is:

git add data/reviewed-auth.jsonl.dvc data/.gitignore

git commit -m "Version authentication review cases"

This commits metadata, not the dataset bytes. Configure an access-controlled DVC remote and push the data before expecting another machine or CI runner to retrieve it. Keep credentials and sensitive review material out of public repositories.

Keep development examples separate from held-out evaluation cases. If developers repeatedly tune prompts against the same questions, those questions become less useful as evidence of generalization.

Split related examples together where appropriate. Near-duplicate questions from one support conversation should not be scattered across development and evaluation sets.

Use two evaluation passes. First, hold the evidence snapshot fixed to compare answer-generation changes. Then test retrieval against the intended document versions to detect stale or irrelevant sources. Record per-item outcomes and report failures by category; an overall improvement can hide a regression in version-specific or multilingual questions.

SitePoint’s article on testing nondeterministic AI behavior provides further context for repeatable evaluation workflows.

A correct answer can still fail in the interface.

Users may overlook a caveat, misunderstand whether an operation has been performed, or miss that the evidence applies to an older API version.

Pair answer review with practical usability tasks:

Find the authentication requirements for version 3 and identify the source that supports them.

Observe whether users complete the task and whether their confidence matches the available evidence.

SitePoint’s overview of user research methods helps distinguish task observation from preference surveys.

Turn interface failures into browser tests: a citation opens the expected document version, its passage ID matches the displayed evidence, missing evidence offers a recovery action, and a failed review submission preserves the draft. Verify that reviewers cannot access unassigned restricted items by changing an ID in the URL. These checks complement human judgments; they do not replace them.

Do not use a fluent response as a substitute for a clear interaction. The interface should distinguish retrieved information, generated interpretation, and actions the application has actually completed.

Start with one authentication task, a versioned rubric, and a small calibration batch. Use Pydantic for ingestion checks, Label Studio or a custom interface for review, scikit-learn for agreement analysis, and Git/DVC for dataset provenance. Add each tool only when it solves a concrete workflow need.

At release time, run deterministic checks for schema, evidence references, permissions, and interface behavior. Compare candidate answers against adjudicated acceptance criteria and send new ambiguous failures back for review. Record the dataset revision and configurations with the results so another developer can reproduce the comparison.

The purpose of human review is to produce evidence the team can act on. A smaller collection of well-defined, traceable judgments is often more useful than a large pile of ratings whose meaning nobody can explain.

#10589. Hello Kitty is selling toilet paper that smells like dessert

Hello Kitty is entering her “Hello Sh*tty” era with a new line of toilet paper that smells like dessert, because apparently no part of your daily routine is safe from Sanrio merchandise.

The beloved Japanese character has appeared on just about everything imaginable, from Burger King kids meals to cans of Spam.

Now, she’s heading somewhere a little less glamorous: your bathroom.

Japanese paper manufacturer Marutomi Seishi has teamed up with Sanrio to release a revamped collection of winter-themed toilet paper, complete with adorable character designs and a rather unusual dessert-inspired fragrance.

According to the company’s November 2 announcement, the Sanrio Characters Winter Toilet Paper comes in packs of 12 double-ply rolls featuring 16 familiar characters.

Hello Kitty is joined by the likes of My Melody, Kuromi, Cinnamoroll, Pompompurin, and Pochacco, all dressed for winter and enjoying an ice-skating adventure.

The toilet paper itself features two different printed designs, with a light purple color scheme that should make even the most ordinary bathroom visit feel a little more magical.

However, the real selling point is the scent.

As SoraNews24 reports, the paper comes with a “sweets fragrance,” meaning your bathroom could smell more like a dessert shop than, well, a bathroom.

The goal is to “create a peaceful, tranquil atmosphere in your bathroom,” Marutomi Seishi says.

Each roll measures 25 meters (82 feet) and is made entirely from recycled paper, so at least it’s environmentally conscious.

Hello Kitty and Cinnamaroll lead new Sanrio sneaker collection

Hello Kitty jumps to Burger King in yet another fast food anime collab

The collection began rolling out across Japan in early November 2026, with availability at supermarkets, drugstores, and online retailers. No official price was listed in the announcement.

Of course, Hello Kitty is no stranger to sweet treats. Earlier this year, McDonald’s Japan released a Hello Kitty strawberry smoothie alongside a Pompompurin pudding frappe.

Now, Sanrio has found a way to bring dessert into the bathroom, too.

It’s certainly one way to make your next trip to the toilet a little sweeter.

#10587. Quantum computer breakthrough tracks qubit fluctuations in real time

Researchers at the Niels Bohr Institute have significantly increased how quickly changes in delicate quantum states can be detected inside a qubit. By combining commercially available hardware with new adaptive measurement techniques, the team can now observe rapid shifts in qubit behavior that were previously impossible to see.

Qubits are the fundamental units of quantum computers, which scientists hope will one day outperform today's most powerful machines. But qubits are extremely sensitive. The materials used to build them often contain tiny defects that scientists still do not fully understand. These microscopic imperfections can shift position hundreds of times per second. As they move, they alter how quickly a qubit loses energy and with it valuable quantum information.

Until recently, standard testing methods took up to a minute to measure qubit performance. That was far too slow to capture these rapid fluctuations. Instead, researchers could only determine an average energy loss rate, masking the true and often unstable behavior of the qubit.

It is somewhat like asking a strong workhorse to pull a plow while obstacles constantly appear in its path faster than anyone can react. The animal may be capable, but unpredictable disruptions make the job much harder.

A research team from the Niels Bohr Institute's Center for Quantum Devices and the Novo Nordisk Foundation Quantum Computing Programme, led by postdoctoral researcher Dr. Fabrizio Berritta, developed a real time adaptive measurement system that tracks changes in the qubit energy loss (relaxation) rate as they occur. The project involved collaboration with scientists from the Norwegian University of Science and Technology, Leiden University, and Chalmers University.

The new approach relies on a fast classical controller that updates its estimate of a qubit's relaxation rate within milliseconds. This matches the natural speed of the fluctuations themselves, rather than lagging seconds or minutes behind as older methods did.

To achieve this, the team used a Field Programmable Gate Array (FPGA), a type of classical processor designed for extremely rapid operations. By running the experiment directly on the FPGA, they could quickly generate a "best guess" of how fast the qubit was losing energy using only a few measurements. This eliminated the need for slower data transfers to a conventional computer.

Programming FPGAs for such specialized tasks can be challenging. Even so, the researchers succeeded in updating the controller's internal Bayesian model after every single qubit measurement. That allowed the system to continually refine its understanding of the qubit's condition in real time.

As a result, the controller now keeps pace with the qubit's changing environment. Measurements and adjustments happen on nearly the same timescale as the fluctuations themselves, making the system roughly one hundred times faster than previously demonstrated.

The work also revealed something new. Scientists did not previously know just how quickly fluctuations occur in superconducting qubits. These experiments have now provided that insight.

Commercial Quantum Hardware Meets Advanced Control

FPGAs have long been used in other scientific and engineering fields. In this case, the researchers used a commercially available FPGA based controller from Quantum Machines called the OPX1000. The system can be programmed in a language similar to Python, which many physicists already use, making it more accessible to research groups worldwide.

The integration of this controller with advanced quantum hardware was made possible through close collaboration between the Niels Bohr Institute research group led by Associate Professor Morten Kjaergaard and Chalmers University, where the quantum processing unit was designed and fabricated. "The controller enables very tight integration between logic, measurements and feedforward: these components made our experiment possible," says Morten Kjærgaard.

Why Real Time Calibration Matters for Quantum Computers

Quantum technologies promise powerful new capabilities, though practical large scale quantum computers are still under development. Progress often comes incrementally, but occasionally major steps forward occur.

By uncovering these previously hidden dynamics, the findings reshape how scientists think about testing and calibrating superconducting quantum processors. With current materials and manufacturing methods, moving toward real time monitoring and adjustment appears essential for improving reliability. The results also highlight the importance of partnerships between academic research and industry, along with creative uses of available technology.

"Nowadays, in quantum processing units in general, the overall performance is not determined by the best qubits, but by the worst ones: those are the ones we need to focus on. The surprise from our work is that a 'good' qubit can turn into a 'bad' one in fractions of a second, rather than minutes or hours.

"With our algorithm, the fast control hardware can pinpoint which qubit is 'good' or 'bad' basically in real time. We can also gather useful statistics on the 'bad` qubits in seconds instead of hours or days.

"We still cannot explain a large fraction of the fluctuations we observe. Understanding and controlling the physics behind such fluctuations in qubit properties will be necessary for scaling quantum processors to a useful size," Fabrizio says.

#10584. Influencer banned from NYC Marathon after stealing French exec’s race number and high-fiving Ariana Grande

An influencer who went viral for high-fiving Ariana Grande during the New York City Marathon has been banned from next year’s race after it emerged that he used a counterfeit race number belonging to a French banking executive.

Content creator Mike Clarke managed to complete the entire 26.2-mile marathon on November 1 despite never officially registering for the event.

According to the New York Post, Clarke created a fake version of bib No. 4207, which had been assigned to Nicolas Bourot, the chief security officer of French banking group BPCE.

Clarke even posted footage of himself high-fiving Ariana Grande near the finish line, with the encounter going viral on social media.

However, his stunt eventually caught up with him when Bourot discovered photographs of the influencer competing under his race number.

Bourot, who had spent 10 years trying to secure a place in the prestigious marathon, was understandably frustrated to discover someone else had used his number.

“It’s not fair for all the runners because it’s very hard to get entrance to this marathon. I waited for 10 years before I was able to. Every year I try, I try, I try,” he told the Post.

A post shared by New Yorkers (@newyorkers)

The French executive initially feared Clarke’s participation could have affected his official results, but his finishing time of 3:23:23 remained intact.

Clarke, meanwhile, admitted in a since-deleted YouTube video that he wasn’t officially registered, but defended his decision to participate.

“At the end of the day they can call it unfair or cheating the system but for me it was to push my limits while running for this goal and have fun while doing it,” he said.

Influencer apologizes for cheating in marathon after being caught riding bike during race

TikToker apologizes after being permabanned from New York Marathon for cheating

Influencer ‘runs’ 5K in seat at World Cup and people aren’t thrilled about it

The influencer claimed he selected the number because of its connection to a friend who died and had dreamed of completing the world’s major marathons. He also said he received a finisher’s medal but gave it to a child afterward.

New York Road Runners confirmed that Clarke had been banned from the 2027 race after receiving multiple reports about his counterfeit bib.

“When individuals fraudulently enter our events, they jeopardize everyone’s safety,” an organization spokesperson said, condemning the behavior as harmful to the running community.

This isn’t the first time an influencer has been punished for breaking the rules at the NYC Marathon. In 2024, TikToker Matt Choi received a lifetime ban after having two people follow him on electric bikes to film his race.

More recently, Chinese fitness influencer Abeeeer lost her Brooks sponsorship after being caught riding a bicycle during the 2026 Berlin Marathon, despite initially celebrating her finishing time as a personal best.

#10583. ChatGPT as a therapist? New study reveals serious ethical risks

As more people seek mental health advice from ChatGPT and other large language models (LLMs), new research suggests these AI chatbots may not be ready for that role. The study found that even when instructed to use established psychotherapy approaches, the systems consistently fail to meet professional ethics standards set by organizations such as the American Psychological Association.

Researchers from Brown University, working closely with mental health professionals, identified repeated patterns of problematic behavior. In testing, chatbots mishandled crisis situations, gave responses that reinforced harmful beliefs about users or others, and used language that created the appearance of empathy without genuine understanding.

"In this work, we present a practitioner-informed framework of 15 ethical risks to demonstrate how LLM counselors violate ethical standards in mental health practice by mapping the model's behavior to specific ethical violations," the researchers wrote in their study. "We call on future work to create ethical, educational and legal standards for LLM counselors -- standards that are reflective of the quality and rigor of care required for human-facilitated psychotherapy."

The findings were presented at the AAAI/ACM Conference on Artificial Intelligence, Ethics and Society. The research team is affiliated with Brown's Center for Technological Responsibility, Reimagination and Redesign.

Zainab Iftikhar, a Ph.D. candidate in computer science at Brown who led the study, set out to examine whether carefully worded prompts could guide AI systems to behave more ethically in mental health settings. Prompts are written instructions designed to steer a model's output without retraining it or adding new data.

"Prompts are instructions that are given to the model to guide its behavior for achieving a specific task," Iftikhar said. "You don't change the underlying model or provide new data, but the prompt helps guide the model's output based on its pre-existing knowledge and learned patterns.

"For example, a user might prompt the model with: 'Act as a cognitive behavioral therapist to help me reframe my thoughts,' or 'Use principles of dialectical behavior therapy to assist me in understanding and managing my emotions.' While these models do not actually perform these therapeutic techniques like a human would, they rather use their learned patterns to generate responses that align with the concepts of CBT or DBT based on the input prompt provided."

People regularly share these prompt strategies on platforms like TikTok, Instagram, and Reddit. Beyond individual experimentation, many consumer facing mental health chatbots are built by applying therapy related prompts to general purpose LLMs. That makes it especially important to understand whether prompting alone can make AI counseling safer.

Testing AI Chatbots in Simulated Counseling

To evaluate the systems, the researchers observed seven trained peer counselors who had experience with cognitive behavioral therapy. These counselors conducted self counseling sessions with AI models prompted to act as CBT therapists. The models tested included versions of OpenAI's GPT Series, Anthropic's Claude, and Meta's Llama.

The team then selected simulated chats based on real human counseling conversations. Three licensed clinical psychologists reviewed those transcripts to flag possible ethical violations.

The analysis uncovered 15 distinct risks grouped into five broad categories:

The Accountability Gap in AI Mental Health

Iftikhar noted that human therapists can also make mistakes. The key difference is oversight.

"For human therapists, there are governing boards and mechanisms for providers to be held professionally liable for mistreatment and malpractice," Iftikhar said. "But when LLM counselors make these violations, there are no established regulatory frameworks."

The researchers emphasize that their findings do not suggest AI has no place in mental health care. Tools powered by artificial intelligence could help expand access, particularly for people who face high costs or limited availability of licensed professionals. However, the study highlights the need for clear safeguards, responsible deployment, and stronger regulatory structures before relying on these systems in high stakes situations.

For now, Iftikhar hopes the work encourages caution.

"If you're talking to a chatbot about mental health, these are some things that people should be looking out for," she said.

Ellie Pavlick, a Brown computer science professor who was not involved in the research, said the study underscores the importance of carefully examining AI systems used in sensitive areas like mental health. Pavlick leads ARIA, a National Science Foundation AI research institute at Brown focused on building trustworthy AI assistants.

"The reality of AI today is that it's far easier to build and deploy systems than to evaluate and understand them," Pavlick said. "This paper required a team of clinical experts and a study that lasted for more than a year in order to demonstrate these risks. Most work in AI today is evaluated using automatic metrics which, by design, are static and lack a human in the loop."

She added that the study could serve as a model for future research aimed at improving safety in AI mental health tools.

"There is a real opportunity for AI to play a role in combating the mental health crisis that our society is facing, but it's of the utmost importance that we take the time to really critique and evaluate our systems every step of the way to avoid doing more harm than good," Pavlick said. "This work offers a good example of what that can look like."

Materials provided by Brown University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10579. Scientists built the hardest AI test ever and the results are surprising

As artificial intelligence systems began scoring extremely high on long used academic benchmarks, researchers noticed a growing issue. The tests that once challenged machines were no longer difficult enough. Well known evaluations such as the Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU) exam, which had previously been seen as demanding, now fail to properly measure the capabilities of today's advanced AI models.

To solve this problem, a worldwide group of nearly 1,000 researchers, including a professor from Texas A&M University, developed a new type of test. Their goal was to build an exam that is broad, difficult, and grounded in expert human knowledge in ways that current AI systems still struggle to handle.

The result is "Humanity's Last Exam" (HLE), a 2,500 question assessment covering mathematics, humanities, natural sciences, ancient languages, and a wide range of highly specialized academic fields. Details of the project appear in a paper published in Nature, and additional information about the exam is available at lastexam.ai.

Among the many contributors is Dr. Tung Nguyen, instructional associate professor in the Department of Computer Science and Engineering at Texas A&M. Nguyen helped write and refine many of the exam questions.

"When AI systems start performing extremely well on human benchmarks, it's tempting to think they're approaching human-level understanding," Nguyen said. "But HLE reminds us that intelligence isn't just about pattern recognition -- it's about depth, context and specialized expertise."

The purpose of the exam was not to trick or defeat human test takers. Instead, the goal was to carefully identify areas where AI systems still fall short.

Specialists from around the world wrote and reviewed the questions included in Humanity's Last Exam. Each problem was carefully designed so it has one clear, verifiable answer. The questions were also crafted to prevent quick solutions through simple internet searches.

The topics come from advanced academic challenges. Some tasks involve translating ancient Palmyrene inscriptions, while others require identifying tiny anatomical structures in birds or analyzing detailed features of Biblical Hebrew pronunciation.

Researchers tested every question against leading AI systems. If any model was able to answer a question correctly, that question was removed from the final exam. This process ensured the test remained just beyond what current AI systems can reliably solve.

Early testing confirmed that the strategy worked. Even powerful AI models struggled with the exam. GPT-4o achieved a score of 2.7 percent, while Claude 3.5 Sonnet reached 4.1 percent. OpenAI's o1 model performed somewhat better with 8 percent. The most capable systems so far, including Gemini 3.1 Pro and Claude Opus 4.6, have reached accuracy levels between about 40 percent and 50 percent.

Nguyen explained that the issue of AI surpassing older tests is more than a technical concern. He contributed 73 of the 2,500 publicly available questions in HLE, the second highest number among contributors, and wrote the most questions related to mathematics and computer science.

"Without accurate assessment tools, policymakers, developers and users risk misinterpreting what AI systems can actually do," he said. "Benchmarks provide the foundation for measuring progress and identifying risks."

According to the research team, high scores on tests originally designed for humans do not necessarily indicate genuine intelligence. Those benchmarks mainly measure how well AI can complete specific tasks created for human learners, rather than capturing deeper understanding.

Despite the dramatic name, Humanity's Last Exam is not meant to suggest that humans are becoming obsolete. Instead, it highlights the large amount of knowledge and expertise that still remains uniquely human.

"This isn't a race against AI," Nguyen said. "It's a method for understanding where these systems are strong and where they struggle. That understanding helps us build safer, more reliable technologies. And, importantly, it reminds us why human expertise still matters."

Humanity's Last Exam is designed to serve as a durable and transparent benchmark for future AI systems. To support that goal, the researchers have released some questions publicly while keeping the majority hidden so that AI models cannot simply memorize the answers.

"For now, Humanity's Last Exam stands as one of the clearest assessments of the gap between AI and human intelligence," Nguyen said, "and despite rapid technological advances, it remains wide."

Nguyen emphasized that the scale of the project demonstrates the value of collaboration across disciplines and countries.

"What made this project extraordinary was the scale," he said. "Experts from nearly every discipline contributed. It wasn't just computer scientists; it was historians, physicists, linguists, medical researchers. That diversity is exactly what exposes the gaps in today's AI systems -- perhaps ironically, it's humans working together."

Materials provided by Texas A&M University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10575. How to Add AI Content Detection to Your Node.js App

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Sooner or later someone on your team asks it. Maybe it’s a product manager, maybe the head of content, maybe a teacher who’s just marked forty near-identical essays: can we tell when people are pasting in ChatGPT output?

The short answer is no, not with certainty. Anyone who promises you that is selling something.

But you can get a useful signal, and you can show it to the people who actually make the call: editors, teachers, moderators. In this tutorial you’ll build a small Express app that takes some text, sends it to an AI detector API, waits for the result, and highlights the sentences that look machine-written.

I’m using the Quetext AI Detector API here. It scores each sentence, not just the whole document, and you can try it without a subscription. Most detection APIs follow the same submit, poll, fetch pattern, though. If you switch providers later, you’ll rewrite one file.

Put the key in an environment variable. Don’t hard-code it, and don’t let it anywhere near the browser.

You’ll make three calls. Analysis takes a few seconds, so the API hands you a ticket and makes you come back for the result. Keep the Quetext API docs open in another tab while you follow along; they list every endpoint, field and error code.

Every request sends your key in an X-API-Key header. The minimum input is 50 words, which sounds annoying until you realise no detector can say anything useful about a two-line comment.

And remember this number: 10 requests every 5 seconds, per account. Progress checks count. It’ll come back to bite you in the production section if you ignore it.

Create quetext.js. I like keeping everything provider-specific in one file, so the rest of the app has no idea who’s doing the detecting.

Three decisions in there that are worth a sentence each:

The errors keep their HTTP status code. A 402 means you’ve run out of credit and a 429 means you’re polling too fast, and you’ll want to handle those differently from “something broke.”

It polls every 2.5 seconds because Quetext’s docs suggest 2 to 3. Polling faster won’t speed the report up. You’ll just burn through the rate limit.

And there’s a hard timeout. Leave it out and one stuck report can hang a request forever. That’s a miserable bug to track down at 11pm.

Next, server.js. It checks the input, runs the three calls, and gives the frontend a simple response that doesn’t mention Quetext anywhere.

Look at the mapping at the end. generated_prob becomes probability, and ai_score comes back as a string like "82.50", so it gets parsed into a number. It’s a small detail. But it means your frontend never learns which provider’s field names it’s dealing with, and that’s what makes swapping providers a one-file change.

Show someone “64% AI” and they’ll jump to a conclusion. Show them a paragraph with three sentences shaded pink and they’ll actually read it.

So the page shows every sentence, coloured by how likely it is to be AI-generated. Create public/index.html:

Run node server.js and open http://localhost:3000. Try two samples: something you wrote yourself, then a raw chatbot answer on the same topic. Seeing which sentences light up teaches you more about how these detectors work than any diagram I could draw.

A quick security point. Those sentences came from your users, then went through an outside service. Treat them as untrusted. That’s why the code uses textContent and never innerHTML, as the OWASP XSS prevention cheat sheet recommends.

The demo keeps an HTTP request open while it polls. Fine on your laptop. Not fine with real traffic.

Submit the text, save the report id with a pending status, and respond straight away. A worker (BullMQ, a cron job, a serverless queue, whatever you already use) does the polling and updates the record once it’s done. The UI says “Checking…” until the result shows up.

Here’s how it goes wrong. It’s Monday morning, 20 editors open their review queues and click “Check” at roughly the same time. Each request polls on its own, and within a few seconds you’ve hit 429s across the board, because the 10-per-5-seconds limit covers your whole account, not each user.

Use one worker that loops through every pending report, with a short pause between calls. Problem solved.

You pay per word, so don’t check every blog comment. Article submissions, assignments and paid freelance work usually justify the cost. Cache results against a hash of the text too. Someone who submits the same draft three times shouldn’t cost you three times.

AI detection doesn’t catch copying. Someone can lift a 2019 blog post word for word and it’ll score 0% AI, because a human wrote it. Just not this human. If originality matters to your product, the same API has plagiarism endpoints that work exactly the same way. The API documentation covers both.

Developers skip this part. It’s also the part that ends up in angry support tickets.

In 2023, Liang and colleagues at Stanford ran essays written by non-native English speakers (all human, written for the TOEFL) through seven AI detectors. On average, the detectors labelled more than 61% of them as AI-generated. Simple vocabulary and textbook grammar read as “predictable,” and predictability is exactly what detectors look for.

Picture an international student getting an automatic zero because a script said so. You don’t want to be the person who built that.

Do that and the detector is a fast first pass that points people at what’s worth a closer look. Your humans still make the call.

You send text, the service checks how predictable each sentence would be to a language model, and you get back a probability that a machine wrote it. Most of these APIs are asynchronous. You submit, get a report ID, poll until it’s ready, then fetch the result. The better ones score individual sentences as well as the whole text, which makes building a sensible review screen much easier.

More than you’d think. Quetext’s API won’t accept fewer than 50 words, and in practice scores settle down once you’re past about 300. A product title or a one-line comment just doesn’t give a detector enough to go on. For short content, either group related pieces together or don’t bother checking it.

No. Detectors make mistakes, and research shows they flag non-native English writers far more often than native speakers. Use the score to decide what a human looks at first, not to block anything. Give reviewers the highlighted sentences, give users a way to explain themselves, and log what you decided. Your users get treated fairly and your support team avoids a pile of angry tickets.

Poll at the speed the provider recommends (every 2 to 3 seconds for Quetext), and remember that those polls count against your limit too. Once you’re in production, move checks into a background queue and let one worker poll every pending report, rather than having each user request poll by itself. When you do get a 429, wait a moment and retry.

No. If the browser calls the API directly, your key ends up in the frontend code where anyone can copy it and spend your credit. Keep the key in an environment variable on your server and send requests through your own endpoint, like the one in this tutorial. That also gives you somewhere to check input length, apply per-user limits and cache results before you pay for anything.

#10572. Scientists discover AI can make humans more creative

Artificial intelligence (AI) is commonly viewed as a technology designed to automate work and potentially replace human labor. However, new research from Swansea University offers a different perspective. The findings suggest that AI can also function as a creative collaborator that encourages exploration, engagement, and inspiration.

Researchers from the University's Computer Science Department carried out one of the largest studies so far examining how people work alongside AI during creative design tasks. More than 800 participants joined an online experiment where they used an AI-supported system to design virtual cars.

Rather than quietly optimizing designs behind the scenes, the system used a method called MAP-Elites to produce visual galleries filled with many different design possibilities. These galleries showed a wide spectrum of car concepts, including highly effective designs, unusual ideas, and even some intentionally flawed options.

Turing Fellow Dr. Sean Walton, Associate Professor of Computer Science and the study's lead author, explained: "People often think of AI as something that speeds up tasks or improves efficiency, but our findings suggest something far more interesting. When people were shown AI-generated design suggestions, they spent more time on the task, produced better designs and felt more involved. It was not just about efficiency. It was about creativity and collaboration."

Why Traditional AI Evaluation May Be Too Limited

The study, published in the ACM journal Transactions on Interactive Intelligent Systems, also highlights a problem with how AI design tools are typically assessed. Standard metrics often focus on simple behaviors, such as how frequently users click on or copy AI suggestions. According to the researchers, these measures overlook important aspects of the experience, including how the technology influences people's thoughts, emotions, and willingness to explore new ideas.

The Swansea researchers argue that AI systems should be evaluated using broader methods that capture these deeper effects. Understanding how AI shapes human thinking and engagement could provide a more complete picture of its impact.

Dr. Walton emphasized that variety in AI-generated output played a crucial role in the experiment. "Our study highlights the importance of diversity in AI output. Participants responded most positively to galleries that included a wide variety of ideas, including bad ones! These helped them move beyond their initial assumptions and explore a broader design space. This structured diversity prevented early fixation and encouraged creative risk-taking.

"As AI becomes increasingly embedded in creative fields, from engineering and architecture to music and game design, understanding how humans and intelligent systems work together is essential. As the technology evolves, the question is not only what AI can do but how it can help us think, create and collaborate more effectively."

Materials provided by Swansea University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10571. Grand Theft Auto is being used to make real roads safer and potentially save lives

Grand Theft Auto is famous for letting players cause absolute chaos on the streets, but researchers are now using GTA 5 to make real-world roads safer and potentially save lives.

From high-speed police chases to smashing through traffic, Rockstar’s open-world franchise has never exactly encouraged responsible driving.

However, according to a new BBC report, scientists around the world have discovered that GTA 5’s detailed environments and realistic physics make it an incredibly useful tool for road safety research.

The game’s traffic systems, weather conditions, and ability to recreate dangerous situations allow researchers to conduct experiments without putting anyone in actual danger.

And unlike expensive professional driving simulators, GTA 5 provides a massive virtual world that’s already built and ready to use.

“Since GTA V does not enforce traffic rules on players, practically any traffic situation can be replicated on purpose,” Julius Schöning, a professor of engineering and computer science, said.

At Osnabrück University of Applied Sciences in Germany, researchers modified GTA 5 to test new safety technology designed to protect cyclists.

One device attaches to bicycle handlebars and vibrates when vehicles approach from behind, increasing in intensity as they get closer. Another prototype uses a vibrating vest to give cyclists a sort of sixth sense for approaching traffic.

Volunteers tested the technology on stationary bicycles while navigating virtual streets in Los Santos, complete with trucks passing dangerously close.

Both devices successfully warned participants of approaching hazards, although neither has become a commercial product.

Meanwhile, researchers at Eindhoven University of Technology in the Netherlands used eye-tracking technology to study how cyclists react to hazards, including car doors suddenly opening and vehicles pulling out of parking spaces.

Game-changing GTA 6 car feature could make crashes way more realistic

GTA 6 makes huge change to iconic controls that might save controllers

GTA 5 Online’s terrible potholes are now saving players’ lives

The findings could eventually help engineers design safer roads and intersections.

Perhaps the most surprising use of GTA 5 involves self-driving cars.

Researchers at the Korea Advanced Institute of Science and Technology have used in-game crashes to train artificial intelligence to recognize dangerous drivers and avoid potential collisions.

Another team at Eastern Michigan University tested an AI decision-making system across nine dangerous scenarios in GTA 5, finding that it could reduce the potential loss of life by approximately 24% in simulations.

While those results don’t establish how the technology would perform on actual roads, they highlight how a game built around reckless driving could help researchers develop safer vehicles.

With GTA 6 launching on November 19 and taking Rockstar’s realism to another level, researchers are already interested in using its massive open world for future experiments.

However, they’ll need Rockstar’s permission before they can start turning Vice City into their next road safety laboratory.

“Its potential as a research tool remains immense,” one researcher said.

#10569. MatPat announces surprise YouTube return with brand-new channel

YouTube star MatPat has announced his return to the platform nearly three years after retiring from his popular Theory channels, revealing plans for an entirely new project alongside his wife, Stephanie.

“What we learned was that one, we loved learning and the channels gave us an excuse to do that. But more importantly, two, that we loved getting to share what we learned with other people,” he said.

Their new channel will feature discussions about internet culture, entertainment, technology, and other subjects that interest them, including the growing popularity of puppet-based content, artificial intelligence, and the entertainment industry.

Despite the growing list of projects, he made it clear that returning to YouTube doesn’t mean returning to the demanding workload he left behind.

“We went for 13 years almost never missing a weekly upload across many channels and this channel is not that,” he explained.

Instead, MatPat and Stephanie plan to release videos whenever they have something they want to share, with some episodes potentially arriving close together and others separated by longer breaks.

The announcement concluded with MatPat reviving his familiar introduction, telling viewers, “For the first time in 2 and 1/2 YEARS. HELLO, INTERNET. IT’S GREAT TO see you again.”

#10567. AI-powered robot learns how to harvest tomatoes more efficiently

Farm labor shortages are pushing agriculture toward greater automation, especially when it comes to harvesting. But not all crops are easy for machines to handle. Tomatoes, for example, grow in clusters, which means a robot must carefully select ripe fruit while leaving unripe ones untouched. This requires precise control and smart decision-making.

To tackle this challenge, Assistant Professor Takuya Fujinaga of Osaka Metropolitan University's Graduate School of Engineering developed a system that trains robots to assess how easy each tomato is to harvest before attempting to pick it.

His approach combines image recognition with statistical analysis to determine the best angle for picking each fruit. The robot analyzes visual details such as the tomato itself, its stems, and whether it is hidden behind leaves or other parts of the plant. These inputs guide the robot in choosing the most effective way to approach and pick the fruit.

From Detection to "Harvest-Ease" Decision-Making

This method shifts away from traditional systems that focus only on detecting and identifying fruit. Instead, Fujinaga introduces what he calls "harvest-ease estimation." "This moves beyond simply asking 'can a robot pick a tomato?' to thinking about 'how likely is a successful pick?', which is more meaningful for real-world farming," he explained.

In testing, the system achieved an 81% success rate, exceeding expectations. About one-quarter of the successful picks came from tomatoes that were harvested from the side after an initial front-facing attempt failed. This indicates the robot can adjust its approach when the first attempt is not successful.

The research underscores how many variables affect robotic harvesting, including how tomatoes cluster, the shape and position of stems, surrounding leaves, and visual obstruction. "This research establishes 'ease of harvesting' as a quantitatively evaluable metric, bringing us one step closer to the realization of agricultural robots that can make informed decisions and act intelligently," Fujinaga said.

Future of Human-Robot Collaboration in Farming

Looking ahead, Fujinaga envisions robots that can independently judge when crops are ready to be picked. "This is expected to usher in a new form of agriculture where robots and humans collaborate," he explained. "Robots will automatically harvest tomatoes that are easy to pick, while humans will handle the more challenging fruits."

The findings were published in Smart Agricultural Technology.

#10564. DNA robots could deliver drugs and hunt viruses inside your body

Imagine tiny robots made from DNA moving through the bloodstream, delivering drugs exactly where they are needed and targeting threats such as cancer cells or viruses. These microscopic machines could also assemble ultra-precise data storage systems and computing devices at the nanometer scale. Although the possibilities are remarkable, most DNA robots today remain in early experimental stages and are better understood as proof of concept rather than practical tools.

Researchers are exploring how DNA can be engineered into working machines using creative design approaches. These include building rigid DNA joints, incorporating flexible components, and using folding techniques inspired by origami. By applying principles from larger-scale robotics such as rigid, compliant and origami robots, scientists are adapting familiar mechanical concepts to the nanoscale. This allows DNA-based systems to carry out controlled and repeatable tasks despite their extremely small size.

Guiding the motion of DNA robots in a constantly shifting molecular environment is a major challenge. To address this, scientists have developed control systems that help these machines behave in predictable ways. One important method involves DNA strand displacement, a biochemical process that enables precise programming of movement using specific DNA sequences labeled as "fuel" and "structure."

In addition to biochemical control, external physical signals such as electric fields, magnetic fields, and light can direct how these robots move. Together, these approaches provide a toolkit for fine-tuning the behavior of DNA machines with a high degree of accuracy.

The potential uses for DNA robots extend well beyond laboratory experiments. In medicine, they could function as "nano-surgeons," locating diseased cells and delivering targeted treatments with precision. Researchers are also exploring whether these machines could capture viruses like SARS-CoV-2, with future systems potentially operating as fully autonomous drug delivery platforms.

DNA robots may also play a role in advanced manufacturing. Acting as programmable templates, they could position nanoparticles with sub-nanometer accuracy. This capability could lead to breakthroughs in molecular computing and highly efficient optical devices that outperform current technologies.

Despite rapid progress, several obstacles remain. Moving from large-scale systems to molecular machines introduces challenges such as Brownian motion, which makes precise control more difficult. Many current DNA robot designs are still relatively simple and operate in isolation, limiting their usefulness in complex real-world environments.

There are also gaps in foundational knowledge. Researchers still lack detailed databases describing the mechanical properties of DNA structures, and simulation tools for predicting behavior at this scale are not yet fully developed.

To overcome these barriers, scientists emphasize the need for collaboration across disciplines. Proposed solutions include creating standardized DNA "parts libraries," using artificial intelligence to improve design and simulation, and advancing bio-manufacturing methods. Progress in these areas will be essential for scaling DNA robots and integrating them into practical applications in healthcare, manufacturing, and beyond.

"The robots of tomorrow won't just be made of metal and plastic," says the research team. "They will be biological, programmable, and intelligent. They will be the tools that allow us to finally master the molecular world."

Materials provided by Journal Center of Harbin Institute of Technology. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10560. GTA 6 boss says physical discs will be the vinyl records of gaming eventually

Take-Two boss Strauss Zelnick says physical games could become the video game equivalent of vinyl records, even as GTA 6 prepares to launch without a disc.

Rockstar’s sequel arrives on November 19, and although players can still buy a boxed copy from a retailer, the case only contains a download code, so they’ll have to download the game just like anyone buying it through their console’s store.

It’s a change some GTA fans have already pushed back against, as many fans hoped for an actual disc.

Now, Zelnick has explained Take-Two’s approach to physical media, confirming that it plans to keep it around, but it will only make up a tiny sliver of the business.

With digital now accounting for 98% of Take-Two’s business, The Game Business asked Zelnick whether GTA 6’s launch marked the end of discs for the publisher.

“It may not be the end,” Zelnick said. “I predicted that digital distribution would be well over 90% of the business more than 10 years ago. I don’t always get it right, but this one I got right.”

“Will there always be some physical business? Yes, in the same way that there’s still a vinyl business in recorded music. But it’s going to be a very small part of the business.”

Zelnick had already hinted at what that could look like for Take-Two. In August, he said discs “don’t really make sense” for some big games because players often have to connect to the internet anyway. He still expected the company to put out physical editions from time to time.

This comes as PlayStation prepares to make a similar move, as Sony has confirmed it will stop producing discs for new games from January 2028.

#10559. AI breakthrough cuts energy use by 100x while boosting accuracy

Artificial intelligence is consuming enormous amounts of electricity in the United States. According to the International Energy Agency, AI systems and data centers used about 415 terawatt hours of power in 2024. That accounts for more than 10% of the country's total electricity production, and demand is projected to double by 2030.

This rapid growth has raised concerns about sustainability. In response, researchers at a School of Engineering have created a proof-of-concept AI system designed to be far more efficient. Their approach could reduce energy use by up to 100 times while also improving performance on tasks.

A Hybrid Approach Called Neuro-Symbolic AI

The research comes from the laboratory of Matthias Scheutz, Karol Family Applied Technology Professor. His team is developing neuro-symbolic AI, which combines traditional neural networks with symbolic reasoning. This method mirrors how people approach problems by breaking them into steps and categories.

The work will be presented at the International Conference of Robotics and Automation in Vienna in May and will appear in the conference proceedings.

Teaching Robots to See, Understand, and Act

Unlike familiar large language models (LLMs) such as ChatGPT and Gemini, the team focuses on AI systems used in robotics. These systems are known as visual-language-action (VLA) models. They extend LLM capabilities by incorporating vision and physical movement.

VLA models take in visual data from cameras and instructions from language, then translate that information into real-world actions. For example, they can control a robot's wheels, arms, or fingers to complete a task.

Why Traditional AI Struggles With Simple Tasks

Conventional VLA systems rely heavily on data and trial-and-error learning. If a robot is asked to stack blocks into a tower, it must first analyze the scene, identify each block, and determine how to place them correctly.

This process often leads to mistakes. Shadows may confuse the system about a block's shape, or the robot may place pieces incorrectly, causing the structure to collapse.

These errors are similar to the problems seen in LLMs. Just as robots can misplace blocks, chatbots can generate false or misleading outputs. Examples include fabricating legal cases or producing images with unrealistic details such as extra fingers.

How Symbolic Reasoning Improves Accuracy and Efficiency

Symbolic reasoning offers a different strategy. Instead of relying only on patterns from data, it uses rules and abstract concepts such as shape and balance. This allows the system to plan more effectively and avoid unnecessary trial and error.

"Like an LLM, VLA models act on statistical results from large training sets of similar scenarios, but that can lead to errors," said Scheutz. "A neuro-symbolic VLA can apply rules that limit the amount of trial and error during learning and get to a solution much faster. Not only does it complete the task much faster, but the time spent on training the system is significantly reduced."

The researchers tested their system using the Tower of Hanoi puzzle, a classic problem that requires careful planning.

The neuro-symbolic VLA achieved a 95% success rate, compared with just 34% for standard systems. When given a more complex version of the puzzle that it had not encountered before, the hybrid system still succeeded 78% of the time. Traditional models failed every attempt.

Training time also dropped sharply. The new system learned the task in only 34 minutes, while conventional models required more than a day and a half.

Massive Energy Savings in Training and Use

Energy consumption was reduced dramatically as well. Training the neuro-symbolic model required only 1% of the energy used by a standard VLA system. During operation, it used just 5% of the energy needed by conventional approaches.

Scheutz compared this inefficiency to everyday AI tools. "These systems are just trying to predict the next word or action in a sequence, but that can be imperfect, and they can come up with inaccurate results or hallucinations. Their energy expense is often disproportionate to the task. For example, when you search on Google, the AI summary at the top of the page consumes up to 100 times more energy than the generation of the website listings."

The Growing Strain of AI on Power Infrastructure

As AI adoption accelerates across industries, demand for computing power continues to climb. Companies are building increasingly large data centers, some of which require hundreds of megawatts of electricity. That level of consumption can exceed the needs of entire small cities.

This trend has sparked a race to expand infrastructure, raising concerns about long-term energy limits.

The researchers suggest that current approaches based on LLMs and VLAs may not be sustainable in the long run. While these systems are powerful, they consume large amounts of energy and can still produce unreliable results.

In contrast, neuro-symbolic AI offers a different direction. By combining learning with structured reasoning, it may provide a more efficient and dependable foundation for future AI systems.

Materials provided by Tufts University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.
