#10457. New programmable photonic chip can control how fast light moves

Researchers at Seoul National University and the University of Seoul have developed a programmable photonic integrated circuit that can slow light whenever needed.

The team was led by Professors Namkyoo Park and Sunkyu Yu of the Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering at Seoul National University, working with Professor Xianji Piao of the School of Electrical and Computer Engineering at the University of Seoul.

Slowing Light Could Help Solve a Computing Bottleneck

The rapid growth of generative AI and large scale AI models has sharply increased the amount of computing power required by data centers and servers. Conventional electronic semiconductors are struggling to keep pace because they consume large amounts of energy and face limits in how quickly they can transmit data.

These challenges have intensified interest in optical computing, which uses light rather than electrical signals to process information. Optical systems could potentially move data at extremely high speeds while using less power.

However, light also presents a major challenge. Because it naturally moves at a fixed speed, it is difficult to delay optical signals or temporarily hold them in place. Those capabilities are essential for creating buffers and memory functions in optical computers.

To address this problem, the researchers designed a programmable photonic circuit that can control both the speed and shape of optical signals. Their approach provides more flexibility over "slow light" than previously proposed methods.

The study was published in the renowned international journal Advanced Science.

Why Optical Signals Sometimes Need To Wait

Photonic integrated circuits are emerging as a promising technology for processing information quickly and efficiently with light. In data centers, optical communication networks, and future computing systems, moving signals rapidly is only part of the challenge.

Systems must also ensure that different signals arrive at the correct time. In some cases, a light signal must be delayed so it can remain synchronized with other information moving through the system.

One method for creating these delays relies on coupled-resonator-induced transparency (CRIT), which uses interference among several optical resonators.

CRIT allows light within a selected frequency range to pass through a device while also reducing the speed at which the optical signal travels.

Traditional CRIT devices usually have operating characteristics that become permanent once they are manufactured. This makes it difficult to change how they function after fabrication.

For example, engineers who want to create a longer signal delay or work with a different frequency range often need to design and manufacture an entirely new photonic device.

That lack of adaptability increases the complexity of optical communication hardware and data center infrastructure. It can also raise costs and extend development schedules whenever new capabilities are needed.

The problem is especially important for AI servers and next-generation data centers, where enormous amounts of information must be processed in real time. Fixed optical components have therefore remained a major obstacle to more practical optical computing systems.

A Programmable Design for Controlling Light

The research team developed a different strategy by treating two optical states in CRIT systems, known as the bright mode and dark mode, as one unified degree of freedom. The researchers also added two controllable loop couplers.

Together, these changes created a new design principle for programmable photonic integrated circuits. Resonator arrangements that were previously locked into one configuration after fabrication could instead be adjusted for different purposes.

Using the new CRIT structure, the researchers showed that the movement of light could be delayed and controlled as needed. They also demonstrated that interference between the bright and dark modes could be handled as a single integrated design parameter.

This approach greatly expanded the flexibility of photonic resonator circuits that had previously been limited by fixed designs.

Controlling Delay, Bandwidth, and Signal Shape

The researchers theoretically demonstrated that the two loop couplers could be used to adjust the bandwidth and shape of the passband. They could also control how long signals were delayed and how efficiently those signals traveled through the circuit.

This means that both the speed and transmission behavior of optical signals could be reconfigured across entire systems containing multiple resonators, rather than only within a single resonator.

Numerical simulations also showed that the speed of optical pulses could be adjusted dynamically while the circuit was operating.

The results indicated that signal delay times could be changed without reducing processing performance. The system could also convert the frequency of light without requiring additional specialized components.

Simulations Suggest the Chip Could Be Practical

The researchers used three-dimensional electromagnetic simulations to test whether the CRIT device could be built on a silicon nitride (Si3N4) photonic integrated circuit platform.

They also evaluated a range of real-world issues that could affect the device during manufacturing and operation. These included material losses, differences in resonator quality, backscattering, coupling fluctuations, phase errors in the loop couplers, and thermal crosstalk.

The simulations indicated that the proposed structure could continue to operate reliably under realistic conditions.

One Chip Could Perform Several Optical Functions

The study introduces a programmable photonic platform that can control both the timing and frequency properties of light signals in real time.

The design could overcome the limitations of conventional optical delay devices, which typically perform only fixed functions. It also suggests that several important capabilities could eventually be combined within one photonic circuit.

These functions include signal synchronization, adjustable delay lines, optical buffers, and frequency conversion.

The same design principles may also be useful beyond CRIT systems. The researchers believe the approach could be applied to a broad range of photonic circuits based on resonators, potentially providing a foundation for more adaptable optical signal processing technologies.

Potential Benefits for AI and Data Centers

If the technology is commercialized, a single programmable optical chip could perform several tasks, including controlling signal speed and switching between different functions.

In that sense, the chip could operate in a way similar to a software-defined system, with its behavior adjusted according to changing needs.

This flexibility could help data centers and AI servers process information more efficiently while reducing energy consumption.

Combining several signal processing functions on one chip could also make optical communication equipment and sensor systems smaller and less expensive.

Over the longer term, the technology could support industries that depend on extremely fast information processing, including autonomous driving, next-generation communications, and quantum technologies.

Researchers Plan Larger Programmable Photonic Systems

Professor Namkyoo Park, co-corresponding author of the study from Seoul National University, stated, "This research is significant in that it proposes a new design principle that allows the flow of light within photonic integrated circuits to be reconfigured as needed, greatly enhancing design flexibility. We plan to expand this technology toward large-scale programmable photonic integrated circuits based on silicon photonics and photonic AI technologies."

Co-first authors Dr. Seungkyun Park and Ph.D. student Beomjoon Chae, who led the theoretical framework and numerical analysis, added, "Through this study, we realized that reinterpreting conventional photonic resonator physics from a different perspective can serve as a starting point for discovering new functionalities in photonic integrated circuits. We plan to further develop this research toward practical device implementation and experimental validation."

Dr. Seungkyun Park is affiliated with the InnoCORE PICORE Center at KAIST and is currently conducting research on photonic AI and quantum optics at the Photonic Systems Laboratory, Seoul National University.

Ph.D. student Beomjoon Chae is conducting research on programmable photonic integrated circuits at the Intelligent Wave Systems Laboratory, SNU.

The research received support from the Ministry of Science and ICT through the Innovative Research Center (IRC) program, the Basic Research Laboratory (BRL) program, and the Young Researcher Program.

Dr. Seungkyun Park also participated in the study with support from the InnoCORE program (PICORE Center).

Materials provided by Seoul National University College of Engineering. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10453. How to Build an AI-Assisted Workflow That Does Not Leak

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A generative AI tool can draft a client email, summarise a forty-message thread or refactor a function in seconds. That speed is real, and it explains how fast these tools have become part of the ordinary working day, used to summarise threads, draft replies and reformat data in a single paste. The same ease is the problem. It moves sensitive material into systems that were never scoped to hold it, quietly and at scale.

Here is the uncomfortable part for anyone running a lean team. The risk in that workflow is almost never the model being wrong. It is that nobody decided what should go into the tool in the first place, and nobody is accountable for where the data ends up. AI made the drafting instant. It did nothing on its own to make delegation safe.

This article is about the control that closes that gap. It is not a product and it is not a policy document. It is a person, placed deliberately in the workflow, making the one call that no model can make for you.

Samsung learned this in public. In 2023, engineers in its semiconductor division were given access to ChatGPT to help debug source code. Within weeks, staff had pasted proprietary code and internal meeting notes into the tool, and Samsung banned generative AI across the company soon after. The story ran everywhere, from Bloomberg to The Register. Nothing malfunctioned. People simply moved sensitive material into a system that was never scoped to hold it, and the material was gone.

Two things make this failure mode easy to hit, and both are structural rather than careless.

First, most of these tools run in the browser. The data loss prevention software a security team relies on was built to watch files, endpoints and network egress, and it often cannot see a paste action into a web app at all. Worse, much of this activity happens through personal accounts that sit outside the controls the organisation does have. The leak does not trip an alarm because nothing was downloaded and nothing was emailed. Text was typed into a box.

Second, consumer-grade accounts can retain what you give them. OpenAI's own documentation states that content from consumer ChatGPT may be used to improve its models unless the user turns that setting off. Paste a client list or a contract clause from the wrong account, and that material may now sit inside a training pipeline you do not control and cannot claw back.

For a developer or a founder, the lesson is not "do not use AI". It is that the point of failure sits one layer above the model, in how the work is handed off and who decided what was safe to hand over.

If any of the data moving through that workflow identifies a person, a client, a candidate, an employee or an investor, then GDPR treats the tools and people touching it as processors acting on your behalf. That sounds abstract until you translate it into design constraints, which is what a technical team can actually work with.

Under Article 28 of the GDPR, anyone who processes personal data for you must do so under a binding contract that sets out what is processed, for what purpose and for how long, and everyone handling that data must be under a confidentiality commitment. An AI vendor you have never papered is a processor you have not accounted for. A free account used quietly by one engineer is a sub-processor nobody signed off. The obligation does not disappear because the tool was convenient.

Location is the second constraint, and it is the one most teams miss. A person, contractor or tool processing your data outside the EU means personal data is being transferred outside the EU, which requires a specific safeguard such as the Standard Contractual Clauses on top of the Article 28 contract. Keep the procesing inside the EU and that entire category of transfer risk falls away before you have to manage it. For UK teams the position is the same: the Information Commissioner's Office sets out the identical core terms for a processor contract, documented instructions, confidentiality, security measures, rules on sub-processors, and deletion or return of data at the end of the engagement.

There is now a second regulatory layer as well. The EU AI Act, which entered into force in 2024, adds obligations on how AI systems are deployed and governed, on top of the data-protection duties GDPR already imposes. The detail is still settling, but the direction is clear: unmanaged, undocumented AI use is getting harder to defend, not easier. None of this is exotic. It is the same governance you would apply to any third party with access to your systems. AI simply introduced a new processor that most workflows never wrote down.

"Human in the loop" usually gets discussed as a quality measure: a person checks the output so errors do not ship. That is true, and it undersells the point badly. The human is also the only part of the workflow that can make a judgment about what should never enter the tool at all.

A model cannot decide that a board deck stays out of a consumer chatbot. A browser extension cannot know that one folder holds client data governed by a contract that forbids sub-processing without written consent. Those are judgment calls, and judgment is precisely the thing you cannot automate. The person doing the delegating is your policy enforcement point, sitting at the exact moment the decision is made. They decide what gets pasted, what gets redacted first, what runs only inside an enterprise account with training switched off, and what a human handles end to end because the risk is simply not worth the minutes saved.

This reframes the division of labour that actually holds up. The AI owns the mechanical layer: summarising a long thread, turning rough notes into a first draft, extracting action items, reformatting data. The human owns the judgment layer: what matters, how it is framed, and, above all, where the data is allowed to go. Remove the human and you have not automated the work. You have removed the only control that was reading the situation and deciding what was safe.

It is worth being specific about what that judgment looks like in practice, because it is a skill and not a job title. Someone exercising it knows which inputs are off limits without having to ask, keeps sensitive material inside controlled systems by default, and treats the question "should this go into the tool at all" as part of the work rather than an afterthought. Whether that person is an engineer, an operations lead or an executive assistant, the control is the same: someone with enough context to know what is sensitive, and enough standing to say no. That habit is what separates a workflow that is genuinely governed from one that merely has a policy document nobody reads.

The practical version, for a founder or a small technical team that wants the speed without the exposure:

Most teams can put the first four in place in about a week. The last two are harder, because they are not configuration changes. They are about whether the workflow still holds when the people in it change, and that is the part most teams discover they got wrong only after someone has already left.

The speed of AI is not in question. What AI cannot supply is the judgment about what should enter it, and the accountability for where the output goes. Treat the human in the loop as a security control, design the workflow around that control, and you keep the nine-second draft without the quiet data leak. Remove the human, and the only thing you have really automated is the leak.

#10451. MIT’s new lidar chip could give self-driving cars a wider view

Lidar technology uses pulses of infrared light to calculate distances and create detailed 3D maps of the surrounding environment. This allows autonomous vehicles to detect objects in their path and respond quickly. However, conventional lidar sensors are often large and costly, and many rely on moving components that can wear out over time. These limitations make the systems difficult to use in a wider range of settings.

MIT researchers have now developed an approach that could lead to smaller, more durable lidar sensors that operate without any moving parts. Their advance centers on a new silicon-photonics chip, a type of semiconductor device that controls light instead of electrical signals.

Existing lidar systems built with silicon-photonics chips usually have a narrow field of view. As a result, they struggle to scan areas located toward the edges of a scene. Previous attempts to expand this viewing range have often introduced extra noise and reduced measurement accuracy.

The MIT team addressed those problems by creating an array of integrated antennas that greatly limits unwanted crosstalk, which occurs when neighboring antennas interfere with one another. The design allows the chip to scan across a broader field of view while producing less noise than other silicon-photonics-based methods.

The advance could support the development of more capable lidar sensors for challenging uses, including autonomous vehicle navigation, aerial mapping, and the monitoring of construction sites.

"The functionality we demonstrated in this work solves a fundamental problem for integrated optical-phased-array technology, enabling future lidar sensors that can achieve significantly higher performance than we could demonstrate previously," says Jelena Notaros, the Robert J. Shillman Career Development Associate Professor of Electrical Engineering and Computer Science (EECS) at MIT, a member of the Research Laboratory of Electronics, and senior author of a paper on this innovation.

The study also includes lead author and EECS graduate student Henry Crawford-Eng, along with EECS graduate students Andres Garcia Coleto, Benjamin M. Mazur, Daniel M. DeSantis, and Tal Sneh. The findings were published recently in Nature Communications.

Many traditional lidar systems use a large rotating unit to direct light pulses across a scene. When the light strikes nearby objects, it reflects back toward the sensor. The returning signals provide the information needed to reconstruct a detailed map of the environment.

Silicon-photonics-based lidar works differently. Rather than rotating a mechanical device, it scans a beam of light electronically in several directions with a system known as an integrated optical phased array (OPA).

At the heart of an OPA is a group of integrated antennas. Each antenna contains tiny, regularly spaced variations along its length. These features, known as corrugations, cause light from an input source to scatter upward and out of the photonic chip.

Researchers can control the direction of the outgoing beam by changing the phase of the light sent to each antenna. Adjusting these phases changes the angle at which the array releases light, making it possible to steer the beam without moving any physical components.

Placing the antennas close together creates a serious obstacle. Neighboring antennas can couple with one another, scrambling the light they produce. Engineers have traditionally prevented this interference by increasing the distance between antennas, but wider spacing creates a different set of problems.

When antennas are too far apart, the array produces several copies of the same beam at different angles. The primary beam can only be moved a limited distance before it becomes difficult to distinguish from these additional copies.

"This limits our field of view, so the autonomous vehicle now only knows what is in front of it for a certain angular range," Garcia Coleto explains.

The unwanted beam copies, called grating lobes, can confuse the sensor and generate false detections. They also consume energy that could otherwise be directed into the main beam.

To overcome this tradeoff, the MIT researchers developed antennas with reduced crosstalk that can be positioned close together without strongly coupling.

In a conventional OPA, every antenna has an identical structure and uses the same pattern of corrugations. When these matching antennas are placed close together, they interact very strongly.

The MIT team instead created a repeating set of three antennas with distinct shapes. They changed the width of the antennas as well as the size and placement of the corrugations. Because the antennas have different geometries, each one also has a different propagation coefficient, which describes how light moves through the structure.

"Because the antennas have very different propagation coefficients, when we put them close together, essentially each antenna doesn't 'see' the antenna next to it. Therefore, it won't couple with its neighbor," Garcia Coleto says.

Making Different Antennas Behave the Same Way

Reducing the coupling was only part of the challenge. Although the antennas needed different propagation coefficients, they still had to release light in the same consistent manner.

The team designed the antennas around three essential requirements.

Each antenna had to emit the same amount of light. Every antenna also needed to release its beam at the same angle when receiving the same wavelength. Finally, the angle of emission had to change evenly across the entire array as the beam was steered.

"We have this challenge where we require the antennas to have different geometries to reduce the crosstalk, but we need to simultaneously design the antennas to have the same emission characteristics. While it is possible to engineer this, it is extremely difficult because, typically, when antennas are designed with different geometries, they tend to behave differently," Crawford-Eng says.

The researchers began by developing the basic electromagnetic theory describing how radiative modes couple. They then used this theoretical framework to guide the design and computer simulation of the antennas.

Based on those calculations, the team manufactured an OPA containing the reduced-crosstalk antennas. The antennas were placed much closer together than those in a conventional system, and the completed device was then tested experimentally.

Interference Falls From About 100 Percent to 1 Percent

Under the conditions of the experiment, a typical OPA would have produced coupling of approximately 100 percent. The MIT design lowered that coupling to about 1 percent while still generating one clean and precise beam.

The system accurately steered the beam across a broad field of view without producing any grating lobes. This combination of wide scanning, low interference, and strong beam quality addresses one of the central obstacles facing integrated lidar technology.

The researchers now plan to refine the method so the system can cover an even broader viewing range. They are also studying another possible approach to wide field-of-view performance that emerged while they were developing the underlying theory.

"This work addresses a longstanding challenge in integrated optical phased arrays: simultaneously achieving both a wide field of view, which requires dense antenna spacing, and high beam quality, which requires low crosstalk between neighboring antennas. The authors solve this problem with an elegant antenna design. Their innovation is an important step forward for chip-scale, solid-state beam-steering technology," says Joyce Poon, professor of electrical and computer engineering at the University of Toronto and director of the Max Planck Institute of Microstructure Physics, who was not involved with this work.

The Semiconductor Research Corporation, the National Science Foundation, an MIT MathWorks Fellowship, the U.S. Department of War, and the MIT Rolf G. Locher Endowed Fellowship supported the research, in part.

Some of the work was carried out using MIT.nano facilities.

Materials provided by Massachusetts Institute of Technology. Original written by Adam Zewe. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10446. World’s first superconducting quantum heat engine could help unlock massive quantum computers

A newly developed superconducting quantum heat engine could deepen our understanding of thermodynamics while helping advance technologies needed for quantum computers with very large numbers of qubits.

Scientists are getting a clearer picture of how thermodynamics behaves in the quantum world, and that progress could benefit both quantum technology and our understanding of familiar thermodynamic principles. Researchers at Aalto University have now taken an important step by demonstrating the first cyclic quantum heat engine built inside a superconducting circuit.

The experiment connects two areas of physics that normally describe very different scales. Quantum mechanics explains the behavior of matter at extremely small scales, even below the size of atoms, while thermodynamics describes how heat and energy behave in much larger systems, from collections of molecules to the universe itself. Bringing the two together raises a fundamental question: what happens to familiar thermodynamic processes when quantum effects such as tunneling, entanglement, and superposition enter the picture?

A Heat Engine Built for the Quantum World

Conventional heat engines turn heat into useful work. James Watt's steam engine is one famous example, but the same basic concept remains central to modern transportation and electricity production, powering cars, ships, planes and many power plants.

The researchers have now created the world's first superconducting quantum heat engine. The extremely small device combines a transmon qubit, a resonator and a quantum refrigerator.

Operating under ultracold quantum conditions, the engine was able to use the tiny amount of available heat to repeatedly produce positive work. Achieving this kind of cyclic operation has been an important objective for researchers working on quantum heat engines. The result provides a proof of concept for superconducting heat engines that could eventually contribute to improved quantum computing technology.

The study, led by Academy Professor Mikko Möttönen, was published in Nature Communications.

Recreating an Otto Cycle Near Absolute Zero

To make the engine operate, the researchers reproduced an Otto cycle inside a superconducting circuit. The Otto cycle is a thermodynamic process also used in car engines and other conventional machines.

"In our experiment, we built a nanofabricated heat engine using superconducting circuits and operated it in a cryostat near absolute zero. At its heart is a transmon qubit, one of the basic building blocks of modern quantum technologies," says Tuomas Uusnäkki, the study's first author.

The researchers connected the transmon qubit to a quantum circuit refrigerator, allowing them to control heat flow on the quantum scale and demonstrate that this heat could be transformed into measurable work. A conventional heat engine normally relies on separate hot and cold environments. In this system, however, the same quantum refrigerator can supply both heating and cooling.

"Our quantum-circuit refrigerator can be tuned to both heat and cool the qubit on demand. Using carefully timed control pulses, we drove the engine in an Otto cycle and monitored the qubit state as the engine ran," explains Uusnäkki.

Measurements showed that heat passing through the qubit during the cycle was producing positive work.

"This is the first experimental demonstration of a cyclic quantum heat engine in superconducting circuits. Using a single controllable quantum refrigerator as both the hot and cold environment of the engine makes it simpler and more versatile," says Uusnäkki.

Toward Autonomous Quantum Computer Hardware

The researchers are now trying to improve the design and eventually develop a fully autonomous heat engine. One possible use would be reading out qubits without having to carry a microwave pulse from millikelvin temperatures all the way to room temperature.

That capability could become especially valuable as quantum computers grow. Autonomous devices integrated directly into superconducting circuits could reduce both the cost and complexity of machines containing very large numbers of qubits.

"Finland's Quantum Technology Strategy envisions a quantum computer with one thousand logical qubits by 2035, which probably means hundreds of thousands of physical qubits. Doing that with current technology requires millions of microwave cables costing thousand euros each. The cables also introduce noise into the system. Using autonomous devices instead would mostly eliminate the need for those cables," Möttönen says.

Reducing the need for those microwave connections could therefore address two challenges at once: the enormous hardware requirements of large quantum computers and the unwanted noise that cables can introduce into quantum systems.

The pioneering experiment was carried out using OtaNano, Finland's national research infrastructure for nano, micro and quantum technology. Funding came from the Research Council of Finland and the Finnish Cultural Foundation.

#10443. How to Learn Java with AI: A Practical Guide for Beginners

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Java has been one of the most in-demand programming languages for over two decades. It powers Android apps, banking systems, enterprise software, and huge backend platforms. The good news: learning it has never been easier. With AI tools like Claude, ChatGPT, and GitHub Copilot, you now have a patient tutor available at any hour.

The catch is that AI can help you learn faster or help you avoid learning at all. This guide shows you how to use it the right way.

Traditionally, learning Java meant reading a textbook, watching videos, and getting stuck on a compiler error for two hours with nobody to ask. AI removes much of that friction:

Instant explanations. Ask "what is the difference between an abstract class and an interface?" and get an answer pitched at your level.

Personalized pace. Ask for the same concept explained three different ways until it clicks.

Error decoding. Paste a confusing stack trace and get a plain-English explanation of what went wrong.

Unlimited practice. Generate fresh exercises on any topic, any time.

But AI is a tutor, not a replacement for practice. You still have to write the code yourself.

Before writing code, you need the JDK (Java Development Kit) and an IDE such as IntelliJ IDEA, Eclipse, or VS Code.

"I'm a complete beginner on Windows. Walk me through installing the JDK and IntelliJ IDEA, and help me run my first Hello World program."

If something goes wrong (and it often does with PATH variables), paste the exact error message and ask what it means.

Use AI to build understanding with analogies and examples.

"Explain Java loops like I'm a beginner. Give me one real-world analogy, then three short examples that get progressively harder."

Then close the chat and write your own loop from memory. That second step is where learning actually happens.

OOP is where many beginners struggle, and where AI shines as a tutor. Concepts like classes, objects, inheritance, polymorphism, encapsulation, and abstraction are easier to grasp when you can ask follow-up questions.

"Teach me inheritance in Java using a Vehicle, Car, and Bike example. Then quiz me with 5 questions, one at a time, and wait for my answer before giving feedback."

Asking the AI to quiz you, rather than just explain, turns passive reading into active recall.

Once you've written a program, ask AI to review it. This is one of the most valuable habits you can build.

"Review my Java code. Point out bugs, style issues, and anything that isn't idiomatic. Explain why each suggestion matters, but don't rewrite the whole thing for me."

That last sentence is important. You want feedback and understanding, not a finished solution you copy and paste.

When your code throws a NullPointerException or ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException, resist the urge to say "fix this." Instead, try:

"Here's my code and the error. Don't give me the answer yet. Ask me guiding questions so I can find the bug myself."

Debugging is a core professional skill. Using AI as a Socratic coach builds that skill instead of bypassing it.

Tutorials only take you so far. Projects are where Java sticks. Ask AI to suggest projects matched to your level:

Beginner: Number guessing game, to-do list in the console, simple calculator

Intermediate: Library management system, bank account simulator, student record manager

Advanced: REST API with Spring Boot, inventory system with a database, a chat application

"I know Java basics and OOP. Suggest a project I can finish in a weekend, break it into milestones, and don't write the code for me."

Copy-pasting without understanding. If you can't explain a line of code, you haven't learned it. Ask the AI to explain it line by line.

Trusting AI blindly. AI can produce code that looks right but is wrong, outdated, or insecure. Always run it, test it, and verify against official documentation.

Skipping the struggle. A little frustration is productive. Try for 15 to 20 minutes before asking for help.

Never writing code from scratch. Regularly practice without AI so you know what you can actually do on your own, especially before job interviews.

AI is a fantastic assistant, but it can't tell you what you don't know you need to learn. A structured course gives you a proven roadmap, so you cover the right topics in the right order, while AI fills in the gaps, answers your questions, and keeps you moving.

The best way to learn Java with AI is to treat it like a good mentor: ask it to explain, challenge, and review, but do the typing yourself. Learners who combine consistent practice with smart AI use progress faster than ever before.

#10441. Scientists tracked kids for 8 years — the screen time result was unexpected

A Finnish study has found an unexpected association between screen use and cognitive performance. Children who accumulated more screen time as they grew up tended to show better cognitive processing during adolescence. One of the researchers cautions against viewing screen time as entirely harmful and says the goal should be to balance physical activity with screen use that encourages active thinking.

Low levels of physical activity among adolescents remain a significant public health concern. Sedentary behavior has been associated with poorer academic achievement, while physical activity is known to support brain function, particularly in adults. Much less is known, however, about how physical activity and sedentary behavior during childhood and adolescence relate to cognitive processing later in adolescence.

These years are especially important because the brain is still developing. Factors that shape cognitive abilities during childhood and adolescence may have lasting effects that extend into adulthood, potentially influencing education and working life.

Researchers examined how physical activity, sedentary behavior, and screen time from childhood through adolescence were related to cognitive processing in the teenage years. They also looked for differences between girls and boys and investigated whether the intensity of physical activity played a role.

More Screen Time Linked to Better Cognitive Processing

The results showed that greater screen time beginning in childhood was associated with better cognitive processing in adolescence.

"The findings suggest that screen time can support children's and adolescents' cognitive processing. Presumably, the essential point here is what kind of things they do in their screen time. Teachers and parents should encourage children to use devices and screens in such ways that promote active thinking, problem-solving, creativity and learning," states Doctoral Researcher Petri Jalanko from the University of Jyväskylä.

The findings suggest that the type of screen activity may be important. Digital activities that involve learning, creativity, problem-solving, or other forms of active mental engagement could potentially help explain the association observed in the study.

"We should not regard screen time solely as harmful but seek balance between physical activity and screen time that promotes active thinking," Jalanko summarizes.

Physical Activity Showed Different Links in Girls and Boys

The relationship between physical activity and cognition was more complicated.

Among girls, greater amounts of light intensity physical activity since childhood were associated with better working memory during adolescence. Among boys, greater participation in guided physical activity from childhood through adolescence was linked to better working memory.

The researchers also found an unexpected pattern involving unsupervised exercise. Lower levels of self-reported unsupervised physical activity were associated with better cognitive processing in adolescence.

By contrast, physical activity and sedentary time measured using a device that tracked heart rate and movement were not associated with cognitive processing. One possible explanation is that these sensors can measure movement and physiological activity but cannot determine exactly what a person is doing during periods of activity or inactivity.

"Our study indicates that the connections of physical activity and sedentary behavior to cognitive processing are complex and depend on the sex, the type and assessment method of physical activity and sedentary time. Moreover, boys and girls may benefit from different types of physical activity in view of brain health," Jalanko adds.

More Research Is Needed to Determine Cause and Effect

The researchers emphasize that the findings show associations rather than proving that screen time or particular types of physical activity directly cause changes in cognitive performance.

"However, we need more intervention studies to find out causal relations and sex differences in this respect. Moreover, it is important to examine more specifically the effects of the intensity of physical activity on changes taking place in cognitive processing."

The results come from an eight-year follow-up of the PANIC study on children's physical activity and nutrition. The current analysis included 124 girls and 136 boys with an average age of 15.8 years.

Researchers measured physical activity and sedentary behavior using both survey questionnaires and a device that combined heart rate and movement measurements. Learning, attention, and working memory were evaluated using the CogState test battery.

The research was published in the journal Pediatric Exercise Science.

#10439. Forza Horizon 6 player still drives with late best friend thanks to his Drivatar

A Forza Horizon 6 player has shared how he still gets to drive alongside his best friend, who died four months ago, whenever his Drivatar appears in the game.

Forza photographer Samohteht posted a clip to Instagram showing the two cars cruising side by side, in a video that has since been viewed more than 2 million times.

Drivatars are AI drivers that learn from the way real players drive, then show up in other people’s games under that player’s name, even when they aren’t online. They have been part of Forza since the first game in 2005, and since Forza Motorsport 5 in 2013 they have appeared in friends’ games too, which is why his friend’s Drivatar still turns up on the road months later.

A post shared by Samohteht | Forza Horizon Photography (@samohteht.fh)

“My best friend passed away four months ago. And every once in a while, I come across his Drivatar in Forza Horizon 6,” Samohteht wrote. “For a little while, we get to cruise together again. No words needed, no goodbye, no sadness. Just two friends driving side by side, like nothing ever happened.”

He added that for a brief moment, “the world feels normal again.”

“I know it’s just a game, but sometimes it’s the closest thing I have to seeing him again,” he continued, before signing off with a message to his friend. “Happy Birthday. I miss you, brother.”

The video prompted other players to share similar experiences in the comments. “Felt this. My best friend’s Drivatar comes around and he’s been gone over 8 years,” one wrote.

“Been like this since FH1 for me and my brother I lost,” another said. “Amazing thing about the Drivatar system is it keeps them alive throughout the games they never got to play.”

Others said the clip had brought them back to the game, with one player who lost his best friend three months ago writing that he was “currently redownloading the game as I speak.”

#10438. Tiny robots powered by light can hunt down and collect bacteria

Robots small enough to operate in the microbial world could give scientists a new way to directly handle objects that are impossible to manipulate by hand. These tiny machines are around 50 times smaller than the diameter of a human hair, bringing researchers closer to the long-pursued goal of interacting directly with the microscopic world.

That capability could be especially useful for biological materials in water, including individual cells and bacteria. Precisely controlling and moving objects at this scale has been a persistent challenge. The newly developed nanorobots show that it is already possible to collect bacteria, transport them, and release them at selected locations.

One of the biggest obstacles in developing machines this small is finding an effective way to propel and control them. At Julius-Maximilians-Universität Würzburg (JMU), a research team led by Professor Bert Hecht has been developing a solution that uses the recoil produced by individual photons to move microscopic devices known as microdrones.

The devices contain as many as four plasmonic nanoantennas. These antennas absorb light with a particular color and helicity, then emit that light in a specific direction. Redirecting each photon creates a tiny recoil force, similar in principle to the recoil produced when a bullet is fired. Because the microdrones have so little mass, those extremely small forces can generate substantial acceleration and speed.

For the latest work, the researchers reduced the size of their light-powered robots even further, producing devices smaller than one micrometer. Simplifying the steering system was an important part of achieving that size while retaining propulsion based on photon recoil.

The new control method takes advantage of nanoscale antenna wires built into the robot. These wires naturally tend to align with the polarization direction of incoming light. By changing the light's polarization, the researchers can control which direction the nanorobot faces. At the same time, photon recoil continues to propel it forward, creating a steering system that works somewhat like the directional control used in larger vehicles.

"In essence, we have built a light-driven nanorobot that can track down and collect bacteria," says Jin Qin, lead experimental scientist of the study. "By simplifying the design, we reached a size at which these robots can operate directly in the microbial world - almost like microscopic cleaning devices."

The robots are also highly maneuverable. They can make extremely fast 90° turns, helping them scan broad areas of a sample in an organized and efficient way. They can also selectively capture, carry, and release substantial numbers of bacteria.

Under controlled laboratory conditions, this means the nanorobots can effectively "clean" microscopic environments. They gather bacteria from one area and deposit them at specifically chosen locations.

"This is a striking example of how light can be used not only to observe the microscopic world, but also to actively shape it," adds Bert Hecht. "The idea of tiny robotic cleaners may sound futuristic, but we are already demonstrating the physical principles that make it possible."

The robots remain fully maneuverable even while carrying larger groups of bacteria, although their speed decreases somewhat under the added load. That ability to keep functioning while transporting larger bacterial clusters points to possible future uses in microbiology, biomedical research, and precise manipulation of materials at the microscopic scale.

#10435. Steam review backlog forces indie developers to delay game releases as AI submissions reportedly clog queue

Steam is facing a growing backlog of game submissions, forcing developers to postpone releases, with some believing AI-generated titles are contributing to the delays.

On November 3, Valve acknowledged that it had seen a “significant increase in submissions for store page and build review in recent months”. The company usually targets a three to five business day review window.

The company shared that it may now take more than twice as long, with around 12 business days required to get around to reviewing submitted games.

Delays have already affected several independent studios, including developer Rami Ismail, whose upcoming game, Australia Did It: End-of-the-World Railway Defense Business, was originally scheduled to launch on November 10.

Its assessment returned a minor controller input issue, which was resolved the same day, but the game remained in the review queue, eventually receiving approval on November 14.

Some believe that AI-generated games may be the cause of the delays. As per MakeTechEasier, Alfred Kamon, game director of Studio Daimon, whose game Food Devils missed its planned early access launch, said: “We can only assume it’s because Steam’s review queue is clogged up by AI slop.”

In response, Valve has introduced changes to its pre-release checklist, including requiring developers to launch their games through Steam and play them for at least five minutes before submitting a build for approval.

Other requirements include checking achievement configurations, ensuring necessary software such as DirectX and Visual C++ is installed, and confirming that Steam Cloud saves work correctly where applicable.

Valve says further improvements to its review process are in development, but has not provided a timeframe for when approval times will return to normal.

#10434. Scientists turn DNA into a memory device that uses 100x less power

DNA serves as the genetic blueprint for every living organism, but it is also an extraordinarily dense way to store information. A single gram can hold about 215 million gigabytes of data. Bringing that remarkable storage capacity into electronics could lead to more efficient data centers, faster processing and systems capable of handling increasingly complex information.

The challenge has been finding a way to make biological DNA function effectively alongside electronic materials. Penn State researchers have now developed an approach designed to overcome that incompatibility.

The work, published in Advanced Functional Materials and the subject of a patent application, relies on two key components. One is synthetic DNA, made from commercially available, chemically engineered molecules arranged into short genetic sequences tailored for specific electronic requirements. The other is crystalline perovskite, a semiconductor already used in technologies including solar cells, lasers and data storage devices.

"Biology and electronics are different domains," said Kavya S. Keremane, co-corresponding author and postdoctoral researcher in materials science and engineering at Penn State. "Bridging these two fields required developing an entirely new materials platform that allows them to function seamlessly together. By combining the information storage capabilities of DNA with the exceptional electronic properties of perovskite semiconductors, we created a bio-hybrid system that fundamentally changes how low-power memory devices can be designed."

Using these materials, the team created a memory resistor, known as a "memristor," that operates with very little energy. Unlike ordinary resistors, which maintain a set resistance to electrical current in devices ranging from cell phones to space shuttles and lose their stored information when power disappears, memristors can preserve a record of previous electrical activity. They can remember the direction in which current previously flowed even after the power source is removed.

That ability allows information to be stored and processed in the same place, resembling the way neurons function in the brain. Such an arrangement could support more simultaneous and sophisticated forms of data processing. According to the researchers, however, practical commercial systems would still require enough storage capacity and electrical power to become costly and inefficient without DNA's ability to pack enormous amounts of information into a very small space while consuming little energy.

"As the demand for artificial intelligence (AI) grows, we need a new strategy for low-power, high-storage devices," said Bed Poudel, co-corresponding author and research professor of materials science and engineering at Penn State.

Poudel said AI and other emerging technologies are expected to depend increasingly on neuromorphic computing, which is designed to operate more like the human brain. Such systems can evaluate multiple inputs at once while making decisions informed by previous experiences and future priorities.

"Usually, it takes more power to store more information. Our device, however, consumes 100 times less power and the storage capacity is higher than traditional storage devices, like flash drives."

To construct the device, the researchers added silver nanoparticles to a layer of customized DNA sequences -- specially designed to be of certain compositions and lengths -- that was integrated with thin films of perovskite.

This technique, called "doping," involves introducing a small amount of another material to produce specific properties. In this case, adding the silver nanoparticles allowed the DNA to conduct electricity while also helping its molecular units line up in a more orderly arrangement.

Synthetic DNA offered another important advantage over natural DNA. Unlike natural DNA -- long, entangled strands that behave like wet spaghetti when handled -- short and rigid pieces of synthetic DNA can be arranged with much greater precision at extremely small scales.

According to co-author Neela H. Yennawar, research professor and director of the Penn State Huck Institutes of the Life Sciences' Biomolecular Interactions Core Facility, molecularly engineered DNA can provide structural organization, adjustable electrical conductivity and functional control that natural DNA cannot achieve when incorporated into thin films.

"We can computationally determine exactly which sequences we need and how long they should be, and then we can rationally design them with synthetic DNA," Yennawar said. "These structures can be systematically doped with silver and other ions and engineered to interface seamlessly with perovskites -- transforming DNA from a biological macromolecule into a programmable, multifunctional nanomaterials platform."

When combined, the silver-doped DNA and perovskite formed bio-hybrid pathways that directed the flow of electrical current through the device.

The researchers found that electrons moved reliably when they applied less than 0.1 volt -- for comparison, standard U.S. outlets have 120 volts -- and the device responded predictably when the direction of the current was changed.

The carefully designed DNA structures, combined with the perovskite, also helped make the device unusually stable. According to the team, it continued operating consistently at temperatures approaching 250 degrees Fahrenheit and remained functional at room temperature for more than six weeks, substantially exceeding the performance standards of existing perovskite-based memory storage devices.

The researchers also reported that the new system could perform the same memory function as comparable technologies while consuming only one-tenth as much power. That level of efficiency could make the approach especially attractive for future electronics designed to handle large amounts of information with lower energy demands.

"Using just the DNA or just perovskite alone did not produce near as robust a result as the combination," Keremane said. "It's this combination that enables a very high memory storage density that requires very little power."

A New Direction for Bio-Inspired Electronics

The team now plans to improve the technology further and explore additional uses for bio-inspired electronic systems.

"Nature has the solution -- we just have to find it and apply it," Poudel said. "This work of integrating DNA into electronics to do amazing things gives a glimpse into what is possible."

In addition to Keremane, Yennawar and Poudel, other Penn State co-authors include co-corresponding author Luyao Zheng, postdoctoral research in materials science and engineering; Haodong Wu, doctoral student in materials science and engineering; Jiamao Zheng, who was a master's student in materials science and engineering at the time of research and has since graduated from Penn State; Shashank Priya, who was a professor of materials science and engineering at the time of research; and Chiranth C. Ravi, who was a master's student in the Huck Institutes of the Life Sciences at the time of research and has since graduated from Penn State. Abhinav Gorthy and co-corresponding author Rashmi Jha, chemical engineering and materials science, University of Minnesota, also contributed.

The U.S. National Science Foundation, the National Institutes of Health, Penn State and the University of Minnesota supported this research.

#10433. Before Dark Souls there was King's Field, and five hours into brilliant homage Queen's Domain, it's already one of my favourite games this year

The clue, of course, is right there in the title. Developer Freshly Baked Games' Queen's Domain isn't just a retro-fantasy action RPG played in first-person; it's an explicit homage to King's Field, the long-dormant FromSoftware series that - as the precursor to Demon's Souls, Dark Souls, Bloodborne, Sekiro, Elden Ring, and all those other Soulslikes - helped shape the modern gaming landscape in its own small way. That, though, is pretty much where my knowledge of the series begins and ends - as much as I love FromSoftware, I've never actually played a King's Field game. I've no idea how Queen's Domain shapes up as Kings Field-like, then, but I do know that, five or so hours in, it's already one of my favourite games this year.

Queen's Domain begins, as many great games do, on a beach. Our hero, Prince Beriik, has been washed ashore after his expedition ship, on a voyage to the mysterious Isle of Neasied, is destroyed. Beriik is searching for his father, King Gius Abraon, who's vanished on a mission to save the kingdom of Bamaen - after years of peace, the evil Queen Siraatsu's army of the damned is stirring; the dead are rising, legendary beasts are returning, and the creatures of the land have turned feral. But for now, there's simply a beach, the cry of distant gulls, and the gentle breaking of surf upon the shore. Queen's Domain's lore might read like an explosion in a vowel factory, but you certainly can't fault its mood.

There's something, perhaps, of the superb Dread Delusion in Queen's Domain; specifically in the way both deploy the lo-fi video game aesthetics of the 90s and early 2000s to invoke a more deliberate kind mood: those unnatural low-poly angles, those shimmering lo-res textures, and those rasterised skies creating - when viewed en masse across an expansive interconnected world - a particularly woozy sort of landscape. Here, though, the vibe isn't so much putrescence as chilly isolation, the muted vistas awash in greens, greys, browns, and blues.

It's a mood sent further off-kilter by the wonderfully odd soundtrack, whose seeping retro synths shift unpredictably from ambient melancholy to thumping dissonance, drawing in everything from parping trumpets to quacking ducks. And then there's the geographic strangeness - the secretly labyrinthine shape of this deceptively compact island - that only grows more apparent the further you explore.

Once you pick yourself off the beach and wander inland, the path almost immediately forks: to the left a boarded trail, to the right a vast stone door. Up ahead a gated cave mouth, and looming high on the clifftop, a mysterious ancient tower. Queen's Domain is full of these kinds of curiosity piquing landmarks, serving as both orientation points and unspoken goals.

Queen's Domain isn't exactly what you'd call forthcoming, you see, and early on - in lieu of a more explicit objective - that tower seems like a promising lead. Rusty blade in hand, you plot a likely course, but while there's a sort of logic to the island from afar, the routes between its landmarks are woven in wonderfully convoluted ways. And before you know it, your path has spilled out onto the coast, winding a perilous cliffside route high above the crashing waves. A second option leads further inland, plunging downward into a shadowy glade. And it's only after battling an onslaught of ladybird-rat hybrids and unnerving chicken-crickets (Queen's Domain loves its weird wildlife amalgams) you realise the two routes converge.

From there, more branches, but always a tangled path; the island's deliberately disorientating design turning navigation and exploration into a fascinating puzzle. And that only increases once you find your first proper sword. Imbued with a powerful darting slash, it's both an offensive tool and - with a break-glass-in-case-of-emergency strafe - an occasionally defensive one. But, it also grants traversal powers, those darting thrusts carrying you across previously impassable gaps. Soon, exploration, initially restricted to ground level, grows increasingly vertiginous, opening up whole new possibilities. You'll bound across treetops, charting a circuitous path around the island, or return to those shattered bridges and see what lies beyond. And that 'what' has frequently managed to surprise me - at one point, an apparently simple ascent turns into a several-hour trawl through a water temple.

Paths aren't always obvious, either; you'll plunge into a pool of deadly miasma, shuttling forward at the last moment to propel yourself along a carefully concealed pipe, or investigate a previously unreachable overhang only to unearth a secret passageway leading to horrifying depths. And often, you'll stumble across sudden shortcuts, causing routes to unexpectedly fold back on themselves. Truthfully, the freedom is mostly an illusion - the island is carefully designed to prevent you from deviating too far - but the proliferation of secret corners, and the minimal handholding, means the paths you're forging feel like your own. And as you unlock new tools, you realise there's a Metroidvania aspect to these spaces too. It's clever stuff, particularly when more overt puzzles are introduced, feeding further into the traversal rhythms.

And throughout it all, combat serves as Queen's Domain's drumbeat, punctuating progress with encounters whose wilfully rudimentary systems do, admittedly, take a bit of getting used to. It's not just the deliberate wooliness of it all as Freshly Baked emulates the combat of video games yore, or enemies' merciless in-your-face lock-on once disturbed, it's the limited ways you can respond. You can swing your sword, step outside of an incoming attack (there's nothing so advanced as a dedicated dodge button), and, well, that's pretty much it. And even accounting for enemies' minimal, largely predictable attack patterns, you'll invariably fumble and die.

But slowly it evolves into something compelling; a methodical game of feints and opportunistic returns, of goading enemies into attacks so you can, as far as your stamina gauge allows, exploit the resulting window. It's still far baggier than, say, Dark Souls, but it finds an engaging rhythm. Partly that's down to a combat twist that actively encourages restraint: standard weapon swings barely do more than chip damage, but hold the button to charge up your attack, and you'll do substantially more harm. That turns combat into a dance of patient timing, as you try and find the perfect moment to unleash a swing as multiple enemies move in.

That's just the foundation though. Soon enough, you've got throwing knives (which slowly replenish over time) for kiting, supplementary damage, or picking off sentry-like foes at a distance. There are spears and bludgeons, often with unusual secondary abilities, and it all feeds into a rock-paper-scissors-style damage system that encourages experimentation with your arsenal - especially where bosses are concerned. And the final flourish is an automatic levelling system that doesn't just strengthen your character as you play, it levels individual weapons - eventually causing them to evolve. It encourages engagement with enemies rather than avoidance, and provides a regular sense of progress in a game where the sometimes unforgiving challenge means inevitable repetition.

Queen's Domain is not, I should stress, a Soulslike, despite some similarities. You don't drop stuff on death, meaning no desperate corpse runs, and while resting and replenishing your energy at crystals resets the world - encouraging some push-your-luck risk-taking - you can save your game independently, creating some welcome breathing room.

Put all this together and Queen's Domain is a wonderful surprise; a moody, sometimes silly retro throwback that, despite its self-imposed mechanical and aesthetic limitations, feels anything but limited. Five hours or so into its supposedly 40 hours of playtime, its mix of combat, exploration, and puzzling has settled into a properly compelling rhythm - and despite some tricky bits and my own cack-handed frustrations, it keeps pulling me back in again. I've no idea if Queen's Domain is a good King's Field homage, but so far at least, it's a bloody good time.

Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS5, Xbox Series X/S

#10431. Top 4 MCP Servers for Marketers: Features, Use Cases, and Limitations

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

You no longer need to build a dashboard every time you want to investigate a drop in traffic or compare campaign results. With servers built on the Model Context Protocol (MCP), you can ask an AI assistant a question and let it retrieve the relevant data directly from your marketing tools.

But connecting an assistant to Google Analytics is not the same as connecting it to HubSpot or Ahrefs. Each server exposes different data, capabilities and levels of access. Some are built for quick analysis inside one platform, while others support reporting across several channels. This guide compares four servers, what they can do, how they are set up and where their limits matter. Details such as plans and tool lists change often, so confirm them against each vendor's documentation before you commit.

The summary below reflects each vendor's documentation at the time of writing.

Google publishes an official, open-source Google Analytics MCP server for GA4. It connects AI assistants to the Admin and Data APIs, so it can list accounts and properties, run standard, realtime, funnel and conversion reports, and inspect custom dimensions and metrics. That makes it useful for investigating traffic changes, landing page performance and engagement trends.

Example prompt: "Which landing pages lost the most engaged sessions this month compared with last month?" The assistant can pull the relevant reports and help you spot changes, and you can then check whether the decline comes from specific channels, countries or devices.

Setup: The server runs locally. Google's documentation lists Python 3.10 or later, pipx or uvx, a Google Cloud project with the Analytics Admin and Data APIs enabled, application default credentials with the read-only Analytics scope, and at least Viewer access on the property you want to query.

Access: Google describes the server as available for read requests only, so it cannot change your Analytics configuration.

Limitations: It covers GA4 only, so it cannot combine website metrics with CRM records or advertising costs on its own. Running it locally also means you maintain the configuration yourself.

Best for: Web analysts and technical marketers who regularly investigate GA4 performance.

Marketing performance usually spans several platforms. Google Ads generates clicks, GA4 tracks site activity and a CRM records leads. Seeing the whole picture means bringing those metrics together.

Coupler is a no-code data integration platform that offers MCP access to prepared business data. It lists more than 400 supported data sources, including Google Ads, Facebook Ads, GA4, HubSpot, LinkedIn Ads and Shopify, and lets teams combine and transform data before an assistant queries it.

Example prompt: "What was our cost per lead across Google Ads, Meta Ads and LinkedIn last quarter?" For paid search specifically, Coulper describes a Google Ads MCP server that lets you ask about campaigns and compare them with other connected channels.

Setup: Coupler is hosted, so there is nothing to install locally. You connect your platforms, configure data flows, choose refresh schedules and connect a compatible AI assistant. According to the vendor, queries run against the prepared datasets, which is useful for recurring reports because teams can reuse refreshed data instead of preparing exports by hand.

Best for: Agencies and teams that need consistent reporting across advertising, website analytics, ecommerce and CRM platforms.

HubSpot's remote MCP server became generally available on April 13, 2026. It connects compatible AI clients to CRM data such as contacts, companies, deals, tickets and activities, and can also read marketing objects such as campaigns, landing pages and blog posts. That helps marketers investigate lead quality, pipeline performance and lifecycle trends without reviewing records one by one.

Example prompt: "Which deals created last quarter originated from webinar leads, and how many closed?" The assistant can retrieve the records and examine conversion patterns, provided the attribution data exists.

Setup: HubSpot hosts the server. In your account you create an MCP auth app under Development, then connect an MCP client that supports OAuth 2.1 with PKCE to the HubSpot endpoint. See HubSpot's remote MCP server documentation for current steps.

Access: Unlike a purely analytical server, HubSpot supports selected create and update actions on records such as contacts, companies, deals, tickets and activities. Other objects, including marketing content, are read-only.

Limitations: Available operations differ by object type, and incomplete lead sources or inconsistent lifecycle stages will weaken any analysis.

Best for: B2B marketers and revenue operations teams that manage customers and pipeline in HubSpot.

Ahrefs offers a hosted MCP server that brings its SEO data into compatible assistants. It supports conversational research into keywords, backlinks, competing pages and organic visibility, so you can follow up with questions instead of exporting report after report.

Example prompt: "Which websites link to both of our main competitors but don't link to us?" The assistant can retrieve backlink data and help you identify outreach targets, which you can then narrow by Domain Rating or organic traffic.

Setup: According to the Ahrefs MCP documentation, the remote server is available on paid plans starting from Lite, and you authorize it through your AI client. Ahrefs also publishes a getting started guide.

Limitations: Calls made through MCP use your monthly API units in the same way as direct API usage, and rows per request depend on your plan. Ahrefs documents a default cap of 200,000 units per month for keys created through OAuth, which workspace admins can change. The data is also SEO-focused rather than first-party conversion analytics.

Best for: SEO specialists, content marketers and link builders who already use Ahrefs.

Yes, but start with the connections your team actually needs. Each additional server adds tools the assistant has to choose between, which increases complexity. Combining Ahrefs and HubSpot, for example, supports separate SEO and CRM investigations, but connecting several servers does not automatically create a reliable cross-platform reporting system.

For recurring reports, prepare consistent datasets and validate the first results against your existing dashboards. That catches errors caused by mismatched metrics or attribution rules.

Are MCP servers safe for marketing data? Security depends on the provider, the authentication method and the permissions you grant. Use trusted servers, review access scopes, and enable write operations only when necessary.

Do I need coding skills to use MCP servers? Not necessarily. Hosted services usually need less configuration, while local servers such as Google's need extra setup.

Which AI clients support MCP? Claude, ChatGPT, Cursor and other tools support MCP connections, though functionality and setup differ.

Do MCP servers cost extra? It depends on the provider. Some require paid plans, and others consume API credits or have usage limits.

Pick the server that matches the question you ask most often: Google Analytics MCP for GA4 behavior, Coupler for cross-channel reporting, HubSpot for CRM and lifecycle analysis, and Ahrefs for SEO research. Start with read-only access, check usage limits and permissions, and validate early answers against the reports you already trust.

#10430. 13-year-old Tekken player beats pro PINYA at Tekken World Tour event

A 13-year-old Tekken 8 player from the Philippines has beaten Japanese pro PINYA at Thaiger Uppercut 2026, one of the stops on this year’s Tekken World Tour.

Martinski, who competes for Team ELITE, picked up the win on the opening day of the Singles tournament on November 14, in a field of 187 players that includes some of the biggest names in the game.

#10428. In Charting the Unknown, Crimson Desert doubles down on its maximalism in audacious, faintly sinister style

And so we are back in Pywel: the super-sized and often super-pretty realm where the deserts are blood-red, the grass is a lurid shade of green, and Kliff and his merry gang of Greymanes seem to be pissed all the time. Yet what's this? Peace seems to have broken out across the region, which means our grizzled action hero is in a reflective mood.

At the outset of Charting the Unknown, Crimson Desert's recently released DLC, I'm tasked with taking Kliff out for a quiet spot of moonlit fishing. The problem is, I seem to have forgotten how to actually fish - and if memory serves, perhaps I never learned because the system was so poorly explained in the base game. So I flail around for a full 15 minutes before eventually relenting and consulting Reddit on how to snag a bite.

You might reasonably assume that these types of basic explanatory wrinkles had been ironed out in the countless updates since Crimson Desert's release in March. Certainly, developer Pearl Abyss has gone to great effort elsewhere to streamline what was, at times, a frustratingly clunky experience (those menus at launch, oh god) while adding even more stuff, such as all-new cutscenes (to improve the story's legibility) and the ability to ride wolves and tigers (because why the hell not). But for all the tinkering and wholesale additions, Crimson Desert has retained its essential character: the menus remain a total faff and the narrative, which I started afresh some weeks ago, is still bafflingly incoherent (maybe even more than I initially appreciated).

It's into this churn of ongoing development that Pearl Abyss has produced Charting the Unknown, a DLC which adds even more elements to a game already buckling under the weight of mechanical overload. Now you can build ships and sail them around Pywel's azure-blue waters. You can dive below the waves and swim with the fishes. You can also fall in love, get married, and - should things not pan out - get divorced. The everything game has become, well, even more everything in quintessentially audacious style. On that note, just wait until you see some of its supermassive Evangelion-esque maritime bosses.

Though don't get too excited. Like the base game, the DLC is a mixed bag. First, shipfaring lacks any kind of drama. Islands are so spread out as to make the actual journey painfully boring: impressionistic oceanic voyaging à la Wind Waker this is not. The diving, though, is beautifully executed. Underwater locales are notably serene, free from many of the irritating distractions that plague ground traversal.

As for the new narrative threads involving all three playable characters - grizzled Kliff, Bayonetta-alike Damiane, and gentle giant orc Oongka - they're equally uneven. Kliff's is the most interesting, transplanting the player to a desert cloaked in a thick orange sandstorm that, without the aid of a working compass, must be mapped out using beacons resembling luminescent dongs. It's pleasingly disorientating, a rare example of Pearl Abyss pushing a kind of intentional design friction, rather than accidentally creating it by inadequately explaining how to interact with the game.

Alas, there are also bugs, one of which was game-halting: Damiane's steed wouldn't spawn when I needed to follow Kliff on horseback. It was a reminder that for all the visual splendour, Crimson Desert can often feel like it's being held together by the code equivalent of gaffer tape.

Everything controls about as well as you'd expect if you've played the base game, i.e. not very well at all. Kliff may be able to run on water and soar through the sky like a cross between Matrix star Neo and Jesus, but he remains a bastard to control for even the most basic tasks. Why can't I lock onto the object a foot away? Why is he doing a three-hit combo somersault through the air when all I tried to do is pull out his lantern? These issues are compounded by the new romance and socialising options that appear every time you target an NPC: Crimson Desert becomes even fiddlier as it introduces these additional layers.

As the hours pass by - as I run, gallop, and bound through the arresting landscape in a manner that is comparatively frictionless compared to the rest of the game - my mind begins to numb. For all the extraneous mechanical gubbins, missions remain brainlessly simple: cavort from one waypoint to another and do a few simple button presses, or crack a few skulls to chug the narrative along. But then perhaps a certain numbing is the point of Crimson Desert? At a time when the real world is markedly inhospitable (diminishing job prospects, the ongoing cost-of-living crisis, the looming threat of geopolitical meltdown), Pearl Abyss' epic swaddles the player like a swords-and-sorcery comfort blanket. It is a safe space to disappear into for hours. Leave your worldly woes at the start screen.

The impression this mash-up of a game appears to want to impart is generosity: here, Pearl Abyss seems to say, is a product capable of accommodating your every wish, will, and whim. It appears to have left no gameplay stone unturned in its ultimate quest to provide the player with total satisfaction - a kind of blissed-out contentment at the thought of never having to leave this almost divine realm.

But rather than generous, perhaps there is something nefarious about Crimson Desert's maximalist design philosophy. It strikes me again that this is the most uniquely modern title I've ever played, as if the Grand Theft Auto and Assassin's Creed franchises, countless MMORPGs (the genre in which Pearl Abyss cut its teeth), and many hyper-compulsive mobile titles were squished together in an attention-devouring final form. One so mightily captivating, so singularly consuming, that the player keeps playing until their lifeforce is depleted and they can play no more.

That seems to be the way these "forever" games curve: toward the player's surrender - of mind, body, and time. And at least until Grand Theft Auto 6 is released in a few weeks, Crimson Desert and its new DLC seem to be the purest expression yet of this type of thing. Wading through its sheer mass of hit-or-miss distractions (which could expand even more), the design ethos begins to grate. As if I'm stuck in an elaborate, endlessly bifurcating maze of hamster wheels, always busy, though never enjoying any discernible pay-off. And so, as its presentational lustre wears off further, and its eye-popping massiveness becomes less of a novelty, Crimson Desert begins to hit differently: much less high-fantasy heaven and more like hell.
